However, normalization also interacts with other techniques (dropout, residual connections) and can introduce its own pitfalls: BatchNorm makes per-example loss interdependent on the batch, and LayerNorm can be sensitive to the position where it is applied relative to nonlinearities and residual connections, as we discuss next.

# Pre-LayerNorm vs Post-LayerNorm residual blocks

Residual connections are fundamental to deep models: they allow information to bypass transformations and prevent degradation in very deep networks. Consider a residual block with input $x$ and a transformation $F(x)$; a canonical residual update is
\[
y = x + F(x).
\]
When normalization is used, it can be placed before the transformation ("Pre-Norm") or after the addition ("Post-Norm"). In transformers, "Pre-LayerNorm" and "Post-LayerNorm" refer to whether the LayerNorm is applied before each sublayer or after adding the residual. These choices have important stability consequences.

Post-LayerNorm residual blocks (original in early Transformer papers) compute
\[
y = \operatorname{LayerNorm}(x + F(x)).
\]
Pre-LayerNorm places layer normalization before the sublayer:
\[
y = x + F(\operatorname{LayerNorm}(x)).
\]
Why does this matter? The core issue is gradient flow and the presence of normalization after the residual sum. In Post-LN, the gradient that flows back through many residual additions must traverse many LayerNorms applied after sums. LayerNorm's normalization depends on activations and hence complicates the backward path: small changes in earlier layers can be scaled or shifted nonlinearly, and in deep stacks this can cause instability during training, especially when using large learning rates or when sequence lengths and batch sizes vary. Practically, Post-LN transformer training can suffer from exploding or vanishing gradients when networks become very deep, unless careful learning rate schedules and initialization are used.

Pre-LN addresses this by normalizing the input to each sublayer. Because each transformation $F$ sees normalized inputs, its outputs tend to have controlled scale, and the residual addition $x + F(\cdot)$ lets gradients pass directly to earlier layers without being immediately squashed or expanded by a post-addition normalization. This yields more stable gradient norms and allows training of very deep transformer variants without special tricks. Empirically, Pre-LayerNorm transformers often converge faster and are easier to train with larger learning rates or deeper stacks.

However, Pre-LN changes the representational properties and may affect the final model's behavior. Post-LN ensures each block's outputs have normalized statistics, which can ease optimization for some architectures. Pre-LN may reduce the implicit regularization effect of Post-LN. To reconcile stability with performance, many modern transformer architectures use Pre-LayerNorm during training and add an additional final LayerNorm at the output, or apply small-scale learnable residual scaling (see next section) to control the magnitude of $F(\cdot)$.

In summary, Pre-LayerNorm improves gradient flow and training stability for deep stacks, whereas Post-LayerNorm enforces normalized outputs at each block. The right choice depends on depth, optimizer, learning rate schedule, and empirical validation.

# Scaling and bias initialization strategies to control gate behavior

Many neural network components use gating nonlinearities: sigmoid gates in LSTMs, the gating in highway networks, attention weight computations, and multiplicative gates in transformers (such as the output of a sigmoid used to scale something). The initialization of biases and scaling of weights determine whether these gates start in a saturated regime (close to 0 or 1) or in a sensitive mid-range (around 0.5 for sigmoids). Properly selected initializations avoid saturating nonlinearities at the start, improving gradient flow.

For sigmoidal gates $\sigma(z) = \frac{1}{1 + \exp(-z)}$, if $z$ is initialized with large magnitude (positive or negative), the gate saturates at 0 or 1 and gradients vanish through the gate. To keep gates "open" and trainable initially, biases are often initialized to small negative values or zero so the initial gate outputs are near 0.5 for symmetric zero-mean $z$ distributions. For example, in LSTM networks it is common to initialize the forget-gate bias $b_f$ to a positive value (e.g., $b_f = 1$ or $2$) so that the forget gate $\sigma(z + b_f)$ starts biased towards retaining memory (closer to 1), which empirically stabilizes training. Conversely, for gates where we prefer them initially closed, a negative bias may be used.

Scaling strategies also help. Weight initializations like Xavier/Glorot or Kaiming/He aim to preserve the variance of activations across layers. Under Xavier for a layer with fan-in $n_{\text{in}}$ and fan-out $n_{\text{out}}$, weights are drawn from a distribution with variance roughly $2/(n_{\text{in}}+n_{\text{out}})$ to keep forward and backward signal variances stable. Kaiming initialization is tailored for ReLU-like activations and sets variance as $2/n_{\text{in}}$. In transformers, the scaled dot-product attention divides the dot product by $\sqrt{d}$ to control magnitude; similarly, some architectures multiply residual branches by small scalars to prevent residual outputs from dominating the identity path initially. For very deep networks, it is common to apply per-layer learnable scaling initialized near zero (e.g., a multiplicative scalar $\alpha$ with $\alpha \approx 10^{-2}$) so that the network initially behaves like the identity mapping and gradually learns nontrivial transforms. This practice reduces initial instability and helps optimization.

Bias initialization can control early numerical dynamic range. Consider a softmax-based classifier whose logits are initialized to large random values; the softmax would be peaked and gradients small for many classes. Zeroing the output layer biases or initializing weights with smaller variance is standard to produce well-behaved initial gradients.

A special case is LayerNorm's scale parameter $\gamma$. In many setups $\gamma$ is initialized to $1$, recovering identity scaling at the start, but sometimes it is initialized to a small value (e.g., $0.1$) to dampen updates early in training. Similarly, attention output projections or feed-forward layer outputs can be scaled down at initialization so the residual connection preserves the input initially.

Proper initialization choices are therefore a combination of variance-preserving weight schemes and bias settings tailored to gate types, along with optional small multiplicative scales that keep layers near identity at the start. These choices reduce the risk of saturation, exploding activations, and poor gradient flow.

# Clipping, scaling and other pragmatic stabilizers used in practice

Beyond the algebraic and architectural fixes discussed above, practitioners use many pragmatic techniques to guard against numerical and optimization instabilities. These methods are especially important when training large models with mixed-precision or on massive datasets.

Gradient clipping is widely used to prevent exploding gradients. Two common variants are gradient norm clipping and value clipping. Norm clipping rescales the whole gradient vector if its Euclidean norm exceeds a threshold $T$:
\[
g \leftarrow g \cdot \min\left(1,\frac{T}{\|g\|_2}\right).
\]
This prevents any single update step from being excessively large and destabilizing parameters. Value clipping clamps individual gradient components to lie within $[-c,c]$, but norm clipping is generally preferred because it preserves the direction of the update.

Loss scaling is essential for mixed-precision training with FP16. Because FP16 has limited dynamic range in the mantissa, gradients may underflow to zero. To prevent this, multiply the loss by a large factor $S$ during the forward pass, compute gradients in FP16, then divide gradients by $S$ before applying updates. Modern frameworks provide automatic mixed precision (AMP) that handles dynamic loss scaling: it increases $S$ until overflow is detected, then reduces it. This keeps gradients in the representable range while avoiding overflow.

Clipping logits and probabilities can also avoid numerical trouble in evaluation. For example, computing $\log p$ where $p$ may be zero due to underflow is hazardous; instead, clamp probabilities to a minimum positive value $\epsilon$ and compute $\log(\max(p,\epsilon))$. While mathematically crude, in practice clamping with a tiny $\epsilon$ (e.g., $10^{-12}$) prevents NaNs.

Epsilon additions in denominators are ubiquitous. Whenever variance appears in a denominator, add a small constant $\epsilon$ to avoid division by zero or tiny numbers. Choose $\epsilon$ consistent with the numerical precision of your platform: $10^{-5}$ or $10^{-8}$ for FP32, and larger if underflow risk is high.

Adaptive gradient methods like Adam include a small $\epsilon$ in the denominator of the update to avoid division by zero in the second moment estimate:
\[
\Delta \theta = -\eta \frac{\hat{m}}{\sqrt{\hat{v}} + \epsilon}.
\]
Careful selection of Adam's $\epsilon$ can materially affect training stability; values of $10^{-8}$ are common for FP32, but for mixed precision some practitioners use larger $\epsilon$ to stabilize updates.

Clip or rescale activations that might grow with depth. For example, when stacking many residual blocks, some implementations multiply the residual branch by a small constant $c$ (learnable or fixed), so the update is $y = x + cF(x)$. Setting $c$ small initially prevents early layers from being overwhelmed and can be annealed or learned during training.

Use numerically stable reductions and accumulate in higher precision. When summing many numbers (e.g., computing a loss across many elements), use pairwise or Kahan summation to reduce rounding error, or accumulate sums in FP32 even if inputs are FP16. Many libraries do reductions in FP32 for this reason. In multi-GPU distributed training, use careful moment accumulation and consistent reduction ordering or compensated summation to avoid non-deterministic drift.

Finally, detect and handle NaNs and Infs proactively. Many training scripts check for NaN/Inf in gradients or parameters and abort or roll back to the last checkpoint, lower the learning rate, reduce batch size, or reinitialize problematic layers. Logging gradient norms and activation statistics can alert you to instability early.

Putting it all together: stabilize exponentials via log-sum-exp, normalize activations with Batch/Layer/GroupNorm depending on architecture and batch size, prefer Pre-LayerNorm in deep transformer stacks, initialize scales and biases to avoid gate saturation and keep early layers near identity, and use pragmatic measures like gradient clipping, loss-scaling for mixed precision, epsilons in denominators, and cautious reduction/accumulation strategies. These practices form a toolbox that lets you translate mathematical models into robust, trainable implementations.

# Summary and practical checklist

Numerical stability is not a single fix but a set of interlocking design decisions. Keep in mind these practical rules:

- Understand floating-point limits: watch for overflow, underflow, and cancellation errors, especially with exponentials and long chains of operations.
- Use algebraic rearrangements like log-sum-exp and max-subtraction in softmax to avoid overflow and preserve precision in log-domain computations.
- Choose normalization appropriate to your architecture: BatchNorm for CNNs with large batches, LayerNorm for transformers and per-token normalization, GroupNorm for small-batch vision settings.
- Prefer Pre-LayerNorm for deep transformer stacks to improve gradient flow, while being mindful that Post-LayerNorm enforces normalized outputs per block.
- Initialize biases and scales to avoid gate saturation and consider small initial residual scaling so layers start close to identity.
- Employ pragmatic stabilizers: gradient clipping, loss scaling for mixed precision, epsilons in denominators, safe reductions, and NaN/Inf monitoring.

Combining theoretical understanding with these practical techniques will let you implement and train complex deep models reliably and interpret numerical issues when they arise.

Introduction

Recurrent neural networks (RNNs) are a fundamental class of models designed to process sequential data. Unlike feedforward networks, which assume input examples are independent, RNNs maintain a hidden state that evolves over time and can carry information across sequence positions. This makes them suitable for tasks such as language modeling, speech recognition, time series prediction, and any problem where past context matters for current predictions. In this chapter we develop the standard "vanilla" RNN formulation, explain how to view the model as an unrolled computational graph over time, and describe how learning is performed by Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT). We analyze the important training challenges—vanishing and exploding gradients—their causes and consequences, and practical techniques to mitigate them. Finally, we consider the expressive power and limitations of simple RNNs and discuss computational and memory trade-offs that arise when training RNNs on long sequences.

# Vanilla RNN formulation and unrolling across time

The simplest recurrent neural network, often called the "vanilla RNN," maintains a hidden state vector that is updated at each time step by combining the current input with the previous hidden state. Let the sequence inputs be $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_T$ where each $x_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_x}$. Let $h_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h}$ denote the hidden state at time $t$ and $y_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_y}$ the output at time $t$ (if outputs are produced at each step). A common formulation is:
$$
h_t = \phi\big(W_{hh} h_{t-1} + W_{xh} x_t + b_h\big),
$$
$$
o_t = W_{ho} h_t + b_o,
$$
$$
y_t = \psi(o_t),
$$
where $W_{hh} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h \times d_h}$ is the recurrent weight matrix, $W_{xh} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h \times d_x}$ maps inputs into hidden space, $W_{ho} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_y \times d_h}$ maps hidden states to outputs, $b_h$ and $b_o$ are bias vectors, $\phi$ is an elementwise nonlinearity for the hidden state (commonly $\tanh$ or ReLU), and $\psi$ is an output nonlinearity (softmax for classification, identity for regression, etc.). The initial hidden state $h_0$ can be set to zeros, learned, or initialized in other ways.

To build intuition, think of $h_t$ as a memory that is updated incrementally. The term $W_{hh} h_{t-1}$ propagates past information forward; the new input $x_t$ modifies the memory via $W_{xh} x_t$. The nonlinearity $\phi$ enables the model to perform nonlinear transformations and to gate or reshape information.

Training and reasoning about the RNN are easiest when we view the model as an unrolled computational graph over time. Unrolling means drawing a copy of the network cell for every time step and connecting them through the hidden states. In the unrolled graph, the parameters $W_{hh}$, $W_{xh}$, and $W_{ho}$ are shared across time steps. The unrolled representation explicitly shows the dependencies of each output and loss on earlier hidden states and inputs, and it is the foundation for deriving gradients in BPTT.

For a sequence of length $T$, the unrolled graph has $T$ nodes (cells) arranged sequentially. The output at time $t$, and any loss contributed at that time, depends on the path through previous states back to $h_0$. Mathematically, if we define some loss terms $\ell_t(y_t, \hat{y}_t)$ at each time step, the total loss for the sequence is $L = \sum_{t=1}^T \ell_t$. The gradient of $L$ with respect to parameters must account for how changes at early times propagate forward and influence later losses—a key reason why training RNNs is delicate.

# Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT) and truncated BPTT

Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT) is the algorithm to compute gradients in RNNs. Conceptually, BPTT applies standard backpropagation to the unrolled network. Because the parameters are shared across time, the gradient with respect to a parameter is the sum (over time) of gradients computed at each time step where that parameter was used, and each of those single-step gradients accounts for the influence of the parameter through future time steps.

To see the mechanics, consider the gradient of the total loss $L$ with respect to the recurrent weights $W_{hh}$. Using the chain rule on the unrolled graph,
$$
\frac{\partial L}{\partial W_{hh}} = \sum_{t=1}^T \frac{\partial L}{\partial h_t} \frac{\partial h_t}{\partial W_{hh}}.
$$
But $\frac{\partial L}{\partial h_t}$ itself aggregates gradients from losses at time $t$ and from later times because $h_t$ affects $h_{t+1}, h_{t+2}, \dots$. One way of writing the recursive relationship is
$$
\delta_t \equiv \frac{\partial L}{\partial h_t} = \frac{\partial \ell_t}{\partial h_t} + \left(\frac{\partial h_{t+1}}{\partial h_t}\right)^\top \delta_{t+1},
$$
with base case $\delta_{T+1} = 0$. Here $\frac{\partial h_{t+1}}{\partial h_t}$ contains the Jacobian of the recurrent update, for the vanilla RNN typically $J_{t+1} = \operatorname{diag}(\phi'(a_{t+1})) W_{hh}$ where $a_{t+1} = W_{hh} h_t + W_{xh} x_{t+1} + b_h$. Thus gradients are propagated backward in time via repeated multiplication by such Jacobian matrices. Implementationally, this is handled by iterating backward from $t = T$ to $1$ and accumulating gradients for parameters.

A practical problem is that sequences in real tasks can be very long (hundreds or thousands of steps), and full BPTT over all time steps can be computationally expensive and memory intensive: you must store all intermediate activations to compute gradients, and the computational cost grows linearly with sequence length. Moreover, because of vanishing/exploding gradient issues (discussed next), contributions from far past to the gradient can be negligible, making full BPTT inefficient.

To mitigate these concerns, practitioners often use truncated BPTT. In truncated BPTT, instead of backpropagating through the entire history, gradients are backpropagated through a fixed window of $k$ time steps. There are two common patterns. In "fixed-window" truncated BPTT you divide a long sequence into segments of length $k$ and perform forward and backward passes inside each segment, carrying the final hidden state of one segment as the initial hidden state of the next during the forward pass but not connecting gradient flows across segment boundaries. In other implementations, one runs the forward pass across many steps but accumulates gradients and then truncates the backward pass every $k$ steps. Truncated BPTT reduces memory usage and computation per parameter update and can serve as a regularizer by ignoring extremely long-range dependencies that the model cannot learn reliably.

However, truncated BPTT introduces a bias: the gradients no longer account for dependencies longer than the truncation window. If the task requires learning long-term dependencies beyond $k$ steps, training may fail. Choosing $k$ is therefore a trade-off between computational cost and the ability to learn long-term relationships.

# Vanishing and exploding gradients: causes and implications

When we propagate gradients backward through time in an RNN, we repeatedly multiply by Jacobian matrices that represent how the hidden state at one time affects the hidden state at the next time. Consider the product of Jacobians from time $t$ to $t+\tau$:
$$
\frac{\partial h_{t+\tau}}{\partial h_t} = \prod_{i=1}^{\tau} \left.\frac{\partial h_{t+i}}{\partial h_{t+i-1}}\right. .
$$
If the norms of these Jacobian matrices are, on average, less than 1 in the sense of their dominant singular values, then the product tends to shrink exponentially with $\tau$, causing gradients propagated from distant future losses back to $h_t$ to become exponentially small. This is the vanishing gradient problem. Conversely, if the dominant singular values are greater than 1, the product can grow exponentially, causing exploding gradients. The core cause is the multiplicative nature of backpropagation through long chains of dependencies.

For a more concrete intuition, consider a linearized version of the RNN around typical activations: $h_t \approx W_{hh} h_{t-1}$. Then
$$
\frac{\partial h_{t+\tau}}{\partial h_t} \approx W_{hh}^\tau.
$$
If the spectral radius $\rho(W_{hh})$ (the largest absolute eigenvalue) is less than 1, then $W_{hh}^\tau \to 0$ as $\tau$ grows, producing vanishing gradients. If $\rho(W_{hh}) > 1$, then $W_{hh}^\tau$ will grow and can lead to exploding gradients. Nonlinearities complicate the picture but do not eliminate this multiplicative effect; elementwise derivatives such as $\phi'(a)$ also contribute factors (for $\tanh$ these derivatives are at most 1 and often less than 1), which tends to push dynamics towards vanishing gradients unless special structure prevents it.

Implications: Vanishing gradients make it difficult for standard RNNs to learn long-range dependencies—information from many steps in the past cannot influence parameter updates for losses far in the future because the gradient signal decays to near zero. This manifests in practice as an inability to learn tasks requiring remembering information across long delays. Exploding gradients produce extremely large parameter updates that destabilize learning, leading to overflow, loss divergence, or model collapse unless mitigated.

From an optimization perspective, vanishing gradients cause early layers (or early time steps) to receive negligible gradient magnitudes, so they either learn extremely slowly or effectively stop learning. Exploding gradients, on the other hand, can cause overshooting and break the assumptions of gradient-based optimization methods. Both phenomena are pervasive when backpropagating through many time steps and are central to understanding RNN training dynamics.

# Techniques to mitigate gradient issues (clipping, initialization)

Several techniques have been developed to mitigate vanishing and exploding gradients. Some address exploding gradients directly, others help alleviate vanishing gradients by modifying architecture or initialization. We cover the most widely used practical methods.

Gradient clipping is the most common technique to control exploding gradients. The idea is simple: if the norm of the gradient (for a parameter vector or for the gradient contribution accumulated over a time window) exceeds a threshold, rescale it to have that threshold norm. Formally, for gradient vector $g$, clipped gradient $g_{\text{clip}}$ is
$$
g_{\text{clip}} = \begin{cases}
g & \text{if } \|g\|_2 \leq \tau,\\
\tau \cdot \dfrac{g}{\|g\|_2} & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
$$
with threshold $\tau > 0$. Clipping stabilizes updates by preventing extremely large changes and works well in practice with stochastic gradient optimizers. An alternative is elementwise clipping (clipping each component to lie in some interval), but norm clipping is often preferred because it preserves gradient direction.

Proper weight initialization helps with both vanishing and exploding gradients. The goal is to start with parameter values that keep the Jacobians near scale 1 so that gradient magnitudes neither vanish nor explode rapidly. For feedforward networks there are well-known initializations (Xavier/Glorot, He). For RNNs, one common heuristic is to initialize $W_{hh}$ to be orthogonal (or approximately orthogonal). Orthogonal matrices have spectral radius 1 and preserve vector norms (up to the effect of nonlinearities), which can help prevent vanishing or exploding dynamics at initialization. More precisely, if $W_{hh}$ is orthogonal and $\phi$ is linear, then $W_{hh}^\tau$ preserves norms; with nonlinear $\phi$, preservation is approximate but can still be beneficial.

Scaling $W_{xh}$ and other non-recurrent weights carefully can also help. Using small initial biases (or special biases, e.g., large forget bias for gated architectures) is another practice that influences dynamics. For activation functions, while $\tanh$ produces bounded activations and derivatives in $(0,1]$, ReLU activations introduce piecewise linear behavior that can help gradient flow because derivatives are 0 or 1 instead of being small numbers like 0.01 in sigmoid saturations. But ReLU RNNs must be handled carefully because ReLU unbounded growth can cause instability.

Architectural solutions are the most effective for vanishing gradients over long time scales. Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) cells introduce gating mechanisms and paths with nearly identity Jacobians that can better propagate gradients across long time steps. For example, the LSTM has a cell state with additive updates gated by input and forget gates, enabling near-linear gradient flow when gates allow. These architectures were explicitly designed to address the vanishing gradient problem and have become standard for many sequence tasks.

Other techniques include using layer normalization or batch normalization (adapted for RNNs) to keep activations in ranges where gradients are stable, and using skip connections across time (residual connections in time) which create shorter gradient paths. Using second-order optimization methods (e.g., natural gradient, K-FAC approximations) can also help by adapting steps to curvature; these methods are more complex and computationally heavier but can mitigate sensitivity to gradient scaling.

Finally, combining truncated BPTT with larger truncation windows, curriculum training, or auxiliary losses that encourage carrying useful information across time can help the model learn long-term dependencies indirectly.

# Expressive power and limitations of simple RNNs

Simple or "vanilla" RNNs are, in principle, Turing-complete under certain conditions: with arbitrary precision and unbounded state, recurrent networks can implement arbitrary computable functions over sequences. More concretely, for rational weights and useful nonlinearities, RNNs can emulate finite-state machines and, with more complex configurations, pushdown automata or even Turing machines under idealized assumptions. However, these theoretical expressivity results are of limited practical importance because they often rely on unrealistic conditions: arbitrarily precise weights, infinite precision arithmetic, and arbitrarily long hidden states.

In practice, vanilla RNNs have several limitations that affect their ability to learn complex sequential patterns. First, as discussed above, learning long-range dependencies is hard due to vanishing gradients; even though the model could represent a long-term dependency in principle, training via gradient descent may fail to find the necessary weights. Second, vanilla RNNs with simple activation functions (e.g., $\tanh$ or sigmoid) have difficulty preserving information over long time scales because the activations saturate and the state can be overwritten easily. ReLU-based RNNs can preserve some information but introduce other issues like dead units or exploding activations.

Another limitation is difficulty in representing hierarchical or compositional structure that appears in language or program-like sequences. While RNNs can approximate many functions, they often need many hidden units and complex training regimes to learn structured algorithms like precise counting or nested dependencies. Architectural modifications such as gating (LSTM, GRU) or external memory (Neural Turing Machines, memory networks) are more effective at learning such behaviors.

From a statistical perspective, RNNs are powerful sequence models but can overfit on small datasets because parameter sharing across time reduces effective sample size but the model can still have many parameters. Regularization techniques (dropout adapted to RNNs, weight decay, early stopping) are necessary in practice.

Finally, the hidden state size $d_h$ limits expressivity: small $d_h$ constrains the capacity to store multiple independent pieces of information. Increasing $d_h$ increases capacity but also computational cost and risk of overfitting. Therefore, practical RNN design must balance model size with the nature of the sequential dependencies one expects to capture.

# Computational/time complexity and memory trade-offs for RNN training

Training RNNs involves trade-offs between computation, memory, and the quality of gradient estimates. Consider a sequence of length $T$ and batch size $B$. For a vanilla RNN with hidden size $d_h$ and input dimension $d_x$, the forward pass at each time step computes $W_{hh} h_{t-1}$ costing $O(d_h^2)$ per time step (if $W_{hh}$ is dense) and $W_{xh} x_t$ costing $O(d_h d_x)$. Over $T$ steps, forward computation is $O(T d_h^2 + T d_h d_x)$. The backward pass through time requires similar complexity; backpropagating gradients also needs $O(T d_h^2)$ operations. Thus per parameter update, computation scales linearly with sequence length $T$—long sequences increase compute time proportionally.

Memory is another constraint. Full BPTT requires storing the activations at every time step that are needed for gradient computation: the hidden states $h_t$, preactivations $a_t$ (for computing $\phi'(a_t)$), and possibly intermediate results of linear transforms. The memory cost is $O(T d_h)$ per sequence (and multiplied by batch size $B$), which can become the dominant resource for long sequences and large hidden sizes. This is why truncated BPTT is often used: by truncating backpropagation to windows of length $k$, one only needs to store activations for $k$ steps at a time, reducing memory to $O(k d_h)$ per sequence.

There are trade-offs between computation and gradient quality. Using smaller truncation windows reduces memory and computation per update but ignores dependencies longer than $k$ steps, possibly biasing learning. Using full BPTT gives unbiased gradients but may be infeasible on long sequences. A compromise is to use truncated BPTT with overlapping windows or to compute gradients with different truncation lengths across training (e.g., occasionally doing longer BPTT passes).

Batching sequences is another practical design choice. For variable-length sequences, padding to a common length allows vectorized computation but increases wasted work; bucketing sequences of similar length reduces waste. Stateful RNN training, where hidden states are carried across mini-batches, can amortize computation when training on very long continuous streams, but it complicates gradient bookkeeping and may require special care with resetting states for independent sequences.

Parallelism is challenging for RNNs because the time steps are sequentially dependent: you cannot compute $h_{t+1}$ before $h_t$ is known. This limits the degree to which training can exploit intra-sequence parallelism. However, parallelism across different sequences in a batch is straightforward and commonly used. Architectural modifications that loosen strict sequential dependence—e.g., transformers using attention—are more parallelizable, which explains their adoption for many sequence tasks despite different inductive biases.

Modeling choices affect complexity: sparse or low-rank parameterizations of $W_{hh}$ can reduce $O(d_h^2)$ costs; shared linear layers or factorized decompositions lower parameter counts. Using more complex cells like LSTM increases per-step computation and memory (additional gates and cell states), but often yields better sample efficiency because they learn long-term dependencies more effectively, potentially reducing the total number of training steps required.

Finally, optimization choices such as gradient clipping add small extra cost (computing norms), while second-order methods add significant overhead but can sometimes reduce the number of required iterations. Practitioners must balance these factors—sequence length, hidden dimensionality, truncation length, batching strategy, and hardware constraints—when designing RNN training pipelines.

Concluding remarks

Vanilla RNNs provide a clear, compact formalism for modeling sequential data, and the unrolled view of computation makes the dynamics and gradient flows explicit. Training with BPTT is a direct application of backpropagation across time, but it brings practical challenges—vanishing and exploding gradients, memory and compute scaling, and difficulties learning long-range dependencies. The field has developed a rich set of mitigation strategies, from simple techniques like gradient clipping and careful initialization to architectural innovations like LSTMs and GRUs, which have become standard tools. Understanding these foundational aspects is essential for reading research papers in sequence modeling, diagnosing training issues, and choosing appropriate models and training regimes for real-world sequence tasks.

Introduction

Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks are a class of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) specifically designed to learn long-range dependencies in sequential data. They were introduced to overcome fundamental training difficulties that standard RNNs face when trained with backpropagation through time (BPTT), most notably the problems of vanishing and exploding gradients. The LSTM architecture introduces an internal memory called the cell state and gating mechanisms that control information flow into, through, and out of that memory. These mechanisms—often called the input, forget, and output gates—allow the network to preserve, update, and expose information over many time steps in a way that is robust to gradient decay. In this chapter we present the canonical LSTM cell equations, explain how the constant error carousel (CEC) and gating mitigate vanishing gradients, survey common LSTM variants and related architectures (including GRU and peephole connections), discuss gate activation choices, examine how recurrent weight matrices mix memory across cells, and summarize empirical behaviors and typical use-cases where LSTMs excel.

# LSTM cell equations: input, forget, output gates and cell state

The canonical LSTM cell augments the standard RNN hidden state with a cell state that acts as an internal memory vector. Let $x_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_x}$ be the input at time $t$, let $h_{t-1} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h}$ be the previous hidden state, and let $c_{t-1} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h}$ be the previous cell state. The LSTM computes a new cell state $c_t$ and hidden state $h_t$ through a set of gates and candidate updates. A common formulation (the "vanilla LSTM") uses the following equations:

- Input gate:
  $$
  i_t = \sigma\big(W_i x_t + U_i h_{t-1} + b_i\big)
  $$

- Forget gate:
  $$
  f_t = \sigma\big(W_f x_t + U_f h_{t-1} + b_f\big)
  $$

- Output gate:
  $$
  o_t = \sigma\big(W_o x_t + U_o h_{t-1} + b_o\big)
  $$

- Candidate cell update (sometimes called cell input or cell proposal):
  $$
  \tilde{c}_t = \tanh\big(W_c x_t + U_c h_{t-1} + b_c\big)
  $$

- Cell state update:
  $$
  c_t = f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde{c}_t
  $$

- Hidden state (cell output):
  $$
  h_t = o_t \odot \tanh(c_t)
  $$

Here $\sigma(\cdot)$ denotes the logistic sigmoid function applied elementwise, $\tanh(\cdot)$ denotes the hyperbolic tangent, and $\odot$ denotes elementwise multiplication. The matrices $W_{\bullet} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h \times d_x}$ connect inputs to gates and candidate states; the matrices $U_{\bullet} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h \times d_h}$ connect the previous hidden state to gates and candidate; and $b_{\bullet} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h}$ are bias vectors. All operations are elementwise across the $d_h$ components unless noted otherwise.

Intuition for the equations: The forget gate $f_t$ decides, elementwise, how much of each dimension of the previous cell state $c_{t-1}$ to keep. If a component of $f_t$ is near $1$, that component of the cell state is preserved; if it is near $0$, that component is reset. The input gate $i_t$ controls how much of the proposed candidate $\tilde{c}_t$ is written into the cell. The candidate $\tilde{c}_t$ proposes new content based on the new input and the previous hidden state. The cell state update linearly combines (elementwise) the retained old content and the new content. Finally the output gate $o_t$ gates how much of the cell state's transformed content (via $\tanh$) becomes the hidden output $h_t$ that is fed to the next time step and to any downstream layers.

Variants on how these equations are implemented exist (for instance, concatenating $x_t$ and $h_{t-1}$ and using a single matrix to compute all gate pre-activations), but the functional roles of the input, forget, and output gates and the additive cell update are the defining features of the canonical LSTM.

# Constant error carousel and how LSTM mitigates vanishing gradients

One of the central motivations for the LSTM architecture is to address the vanishing gradient problem encountered in training RNNs via BPTT. In standard RNNs, the hidden state $h_t$ typically depends on $h_{t-1}$ through a nonlinear transformation such as $h_t = \phi(W x_t + U h_{t-1})$. When backpropagating gradients across many time steps, repeated multiplication by the Jacobian matrices $\partial h_t/\partial h_{t-1}$ often causes gradients to shrink exponentially (vanishing) or grow exponentially (exploding), making long-range learning difficult.

The LSTM's cell state $c_t$ introduces a path for error signals that is, under idealized settings, nearly linear and thus avoids multiplicative shrinking. Consider the cell update

$$
c_t = f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde{c}_t.
$$

If the forget gate $f_t$ is set to $1$ and the input gate $i_t$ is set to $0$ for a particular dimension over a stretch of time steps, that component of the cell state simply propagates unchanged: $c_t = c_{t-1} = \cdots$. During backpropagation, the derivative of $c_t$ with respect to $c_{t-1}$ along that dimension is $1$, so gradients can flow through many time steps without vanishing. This mechanism is called the constant error carousel (CEC) because the cell provides a path where the error signal can circulate unchanged, in contrast to the repeated nonlinear contractions of a simple RNN.

More generally, even when gates are not exactly $0$ or $1$, the multiplicative factor affecting the gradient through time is the forget gate $f_t$ (elementwise). The gradient flowing to $c_{t-1}$ from $c_t$ is

$$
\frac{\partial L}{\partial c_{t-1}} = \frac{\partial L}{\partial c_t} \odot f_t + \text{other terms},
$$

so controlling $f_t$ allows the network to control the magnitude of gradient propagation. Because $f_t$ is computed adaptively by a sigmoid from inputs and previous hidden states, the model can learn to keep $f_t$ near $1$ when long-term memory retention is needed and near $0$ when forgetting is appropriate. The presence of additive connections (the sum $f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde{c}_t$) is crucial: additive updates avoid repeated multiplication by values with magnitude less than 1, which is what causes vanishing gradients in standard RNNs. The gating nonlinearity then modulates the effective gradient but does so in a controlled, learnable manner.

It is also instructive to consider the path from a distant cell state $c_k$ to a later loss $L$ at time $t$ ($t > k$). The partial derivative $\partial L / \partial c_k$ will involve a product of forget gate values $\prod_{j=k+1}^{t} f_j$ along that path, but because $f_j$ are learned and can be close to $1$, the product need not vanish. In practice, LSTM units often learn to maintain relevant information by keeping certain dimensions in the cell state that experience little decay, enabling networks to remember information across many time steps. This is the core mechanism by which LSTMs mitigate vanishing gradients.

# Common LSTM variants (peephole connections, GRU overview) and multi-cell/vectorized LSTM

Since the original LSTM formulation, many variants and simplifications have been proposed. Some modify connectivity (e.g., peephole connections), some collapse gates for fewer parameters (e.g., the Gated Recurrent Unit), and others change how multiple memory cells are arranged or projected. We review key variants and their motivations.

Peephole connections
Peephole LSTMs add direct connections from the cell state $c_{t-1}$ (or $c_t$) to the gate pre-activations. For example, with peepholes one might use

$$
i_t = \sigma(W_i x_t + U_i h_{t-1} + V_i c_{t-1} + b_i),
$$

$$
f_t = \sigma(W_f x_t + U_f h_{t-1} + V_f c_{t-1} + b_f),
$$

$$
o_t = \sigma(W_o x_t + U_o h_{t-1} + V_o c_t + b_o),
$$

where $V_{\bullet}$ are diagonal (often) matrices or vectors that gate cell-state information into gate computations. The motivation is that gate decisions might benefit from observing the actual cell content, not only the previous hidden state. Peephole connections can help in tasks requiring precise timing (for instance, counting) because the gates can directly inspect the memory content when deciding to open or close.

Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU)
The GRU is a popular simplification that merges the input and forget gates into a single "update" gate and combines the cell and hidden states. A standard GRU computes:

$$
z_t = \sigma(W_z x_t + U_z h_{t-1} + b_z)
$$

$$
r_t = \sigma(W_r x_t + U_r h_{t-1} + b_r)
$$

$$
\tilde{h}_t = \tanh(W_h x_t + U_h (r_t \odot h_{t-1}) + b_h)
$$

$$
h_t = z_t \odot h_{t-1} + (1 - z_t) \odot \tilde{h}_t.
$$

Here $z_t$ is the update gate (analogous to "how much to keep" like the forget gate), $r_t$ is the reset gate (analogous to how much of the previous state to expose to the candidate computation), and $\tilde{h}_t$ is the candidate state. GRUs are simpler (fewer parameters) and often perform comparably to LSTMs; in some tasks they train faster, though LSTMs sometimes outperform GRUs on tasks requiring very precise long-range memory. The GRU does not maintain a separate cell state $c_t$; instead, $h_t$ plays both roles.

Multi-cell and vectorized LSTM
The canonical description treats the cell state as a vector with elementwise gating: each component is an independent memory slot with its own gates. This is what we mean by "vectorized LSTM": all gates operate elementwise over the $d_h$ dimensions. However, some architectures consider more structured multi-cell arrangements where groups of cells share gates or where each unit contains multiple internal memory cells whose contents are mixed. For example, in architecture search or specialized hardware designs one might allocate several scalar memory elements per unit and apply learned linear combinations (mixing) to produce the outputs or to produce gate preactivations. The idea is to increase representational capacity or allow richer intra-unit dynamics while controlling parameter count. Another variant is to add a projection layer: compute a high-dimensional cell state $c_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_c}$ but then produce a lower-dimensional hidden state $h_t = P \cdot (o_t \odot \tanh(c_t))$ with $P \in \mathbb{R}^{d_h \times d_c}$, which reduces recurrent parameter size while preserving memory capacity.

Other notable variants include:

- Coupled input-forget gates: replace separate $i_t$ and $f_t$ with a single gate (as explored in some LSTM simplifications), e.g., set $f_t = 1 - i_t$.
- Layer normalization or batch normalization applied to gate preactivations, which stabilize and speed up training.
- Zoneout and recurrent dropout variants that regularize the memory state by randomly preserving previous activations.
- Chrono initialization and bias initialization of the forget gate to favor long-term retention early in training (e.g., initializing $b_f$ to a positive value).

Each variant alters the dynamics and inductive biases of the memory. Selecting a variant is often empirical: peephole connections can help where timing matters; projection layers help scale; GRUs provide a compact alternative.

# Gate activation functions and their role

Gate activation functions determine how gate pre-activations map to gating factors that modulate the flow of information through the cell. In the canonical LSTM, gates use the sigmoid function $\sigma(z) = 1 / (1 + e^{-z})$, which maps real-valued pre-activations into the interval $(0,1)$, appropriate for interpolation weights. The candidate cell update typically uses $\tanh(z)$ to produce values in $(-1,1)$, supplying new content that can be added into the cell. The output $h_t$ is often a gated $\tanh(c_t)$ so that the hidden state remains bounded.

Why these functions? There are several considerations:

- Range suitability: The gates act as elementwise multiplicative coefficients on cell contents, so outputs in $[0,1]$ are natural because they can scale or fully pass/block signals. The sigmoid is a simple choice that yields continuous, differentiable gating factors.
  
- Smoothness and gradient flow: Both sigmoid and $\tanh$ are smooth and differentiable, which is necessary for gradient-based training. However, note that sigmoid and $\tanh$ have saturating tails (their derivatives approach 0 for large magnitude inputs), which can still cause gradient shrinkage if pre-activations become too large in magnitude. That is why initialization, normalization, and bias choices (for example, initializing forget-bias $b_f$ positive) are important to keep gates in useful, non-saturated regimes during early training.

- Alternatives: Some works use hard-sigmoid or hard-tanh approximations to speed up computation and reduce saturation effects, or rectified linear units (ReLUs) in candidate computations. Hard-sigmoid approximations clip linear preactivations into $[0,1]$ more aggressively and have piecewise-linear derivatives, which can help optimization but introduce non-smoothness at breakpoints.

- Output nonlinearity: The $\tanh$ on $c_t$ before applying $o_t$ bounds the passed values and keeps $h_t$ in a reasonable range. Some implementations omit the $\tanh$ or replace it with other functions depending on downstream needs; however, unbounded outputs can destabilize recurrent dynamics, so bounding is common.

Gate activation functions therefore craft the dynamic range and differentiability of the gates. Practically, the combination of sigmoid gates and $\tanh$ candidates provides an effective and stable mechanism for controlling information flow; when needed, careful initialization or normalization can mitigate saturation and maintain useful gradient magnitudes.

# Recurrent weight matrices and memory mixing across cells

In the LSTM equations, the recurrent matrices $U_{\bullet}$ control how the previous hidden state $h_{t-1}$ affects gate activations and candidate updates. These matrices are fundamental to how information stored across different cell components is mixed and how temporal patterns are recognized. Understanding their role is important for both theory and practice.

Mixing across cells vs. elementwise independence
The canonical LSTM treats the cell state $c_t$ as a vector of slots, and the elementwise gating means that, absent recurrent mixing, each slot could act independently. But the recurrent matrices $U_c$, $U_i$, $U_f$, and $U_o$ introduce coupling across dimensions: each gate’s pre-activation $W_{\bullet} x_t + U_{\bullet} h_{t-1}$ aggregates contributions from all dimensions of $h_{t-1}$. Because $h_{t-1}$ itself is a gated nonlinear transform of $c_{t-1}$, the recurrent matrices allow the network to compute cross-slot functions, such as "if memory dimension 3 is active then open gate 5", thereby orchestrating coordinated memory updates and reads.

If the recurrent matrices are full and dense, the gates can route information arbitrarily among slots. For some tasks, this is necessary: long-range dependencies often require transformations that mix multiple signals. For other tasks, especially where specialized independent counters or registers are desirable, one might prefer sparse or block-diagonal recurrent matrices, or architectures that enforce limited mixing. In practice, full matrices are the default because they maximize expressivity.

Initialization and stability
The eigenvalues of recurrent matrices influence how information and gradients are transformed across steps. If the effective linearized recurrent dynamics have eigenvalues with magnitude much greater than 1, gradients can explode and activations can blow up. If eigenvalues are much less than 1, information decays quickly. Techniques that help include orthogonal initialization of recurrent matrices (which preserves norm under linear evolution, in the absence of nonlinearities), scaled identity initialization for some recurrent weights, and careful bias initialization (e.g., forget-bias initialization) to shape short-term dynamics.

Memory mixing in multi-layer and bidirectional stacks
Stacking LSTM layers compounds memory mixing: the hidden state passed upward mixes across cells in the lower layer through learned matrices in the upper layer. Bidirectional LSTMs create forward and backward recurrence that jointly allow each time step to access context from both past and future, effectively mixing information across time directions. Projection layers (low-rank factorization of recurrent maps) can reduce parameter count while still allowing mixing through a smaller-dimensional recurrent hidden state.

Regularization and structured mixing
To prevent overfitting or to induce interpretable structures, one may regularize recurrent matrices (e.g., weight decay, spectral norm constraints) or use sparsity penalties. Structured parameterizations—block-sparse matrices, low-rank factorizations, or grouped connections—control how memory is mixed and can yield computational or inductive benefits. In sequence-to-sequence architectures, attention mechanisms often take over explicit long-range routing duties, reducing the need for extreme recurrent mixing.

In summary, recurrent matrices are the learned mechanism that maps past hidden states into gate decisions and candidate proposals, and thus they determine how memory elements coordinate and transform over time. Their initialization, structure, and regularization have strong practical impact on learning dynamics and representational behavior.

# Empirical behaviors and typical use-cases of LSTMs

LSTMs have become a go-to model for many sequence modeling tasks because they reliably learn temporal dependencies that simple RNNs cannot. However, their empirical behaviors and best-use practices are nuanced.

Typical use-cases
LSTMs excel when the data has sequential structure with long-range dependencies that are not easily captured by fixed-window models. Common application domains include natural language processing (language modeling, machine translation, named entity recognition, sequence tagging), speech recognition and synthesis, time-series forecasting, video frame prediction, and signal processing. In many sequence-to-sequence tasks (e.g., machine translation), encoder-decoder architectures with LSTM encoders and decoders were the dominant approach before attention-only transformer models gained prominence.

Behavior on short vs. long sequences
On short sequences with limited context dependencies, LSTMs often match or slightly outperform simpler models and are robust off-the-shelf. On very long sequences, LSTMs can retain relevant signals if trained with appropriate initialization and regularization, but their ability to access arbitrarily distant tokens is limited by data scarcity, optimization challenges, and the need to allocate specific memory slots. Attention mechanisms or memory-augmented architectures are often preferred when arbitrary, content-based access to long contexts is required.

Learning dynamics and practical tips
LSTMs can be sensitive to hyperparameters. Important practical considerations include:

- Forget gate bias initialization: Initializing the forget bias $b_f$ to a positive value (e.g., 1 or 2) encourages $f_t$ to be closer to 1 at the start of training, biasing the model toward remembering rather than forgetting early on. This often improves performance on tasks requiring longer memory.

- Gradient clipping: Because recurrent models can still produce exploding gradients, clipping gradients by global norm or value helps stabilize training.

- Layer normalization / recurrent batch norm: Normalizing gate pre-activations or hidden states can speed convergence and reduce sensitivity to learning rates.

- Dropout strategies: Applying dropout to inputs and non-recurrent connections (variational dropout or zoneout for recurrent connections) helps regularize without destroying temporal coherence.

- Learning rate schedules and optimizers: Adaptive optimizers like Adam are popular, but SGD with momentum can generalize well. Learning rate decay or warm restarts can help.

Interpretability and failure modes
LSTM cells can learn interpretable behaviors—e.g., units that act as counters, detectors that turn on when specific patterns occur, or units that store syntactic state in language tasks. Researchers have used visualization and probing techniques to identify such units. Nevertheless, interpreting LSTMs is not trivial: representations are distributed, and many behaviors arise from interactions of gates and recurrent mixing.

Comparison to other architectures
LSTMs remained state-of-the-art for many sequence tasks for years, but newer architectures like the Transformer (based on self-attention) can model long-range dependencies more directly and allow more parallelism during training. Still, LSTMs remain competitive in settings with streaming data, where causal, online processing is required, or where limited compute or model size makes recurrent approaches preferable. GRUs are a lighter-weight alternative when parameter or compute budgets are tight.

When to use LSTMs
Use LSTMs if your task involves sequential, time-evolving data where (a) you need to process sequences online or in streaming fashion, (b) you expect to benefit from learned gating and stateful memory without the inductive overhead of attention, or (c) you have modest sequence lengths and want a robust baseline. Consider GRUs for a simpler, faster baseline. Consider attention or transformer-style models when you need direct, content-based access to very long contexts and can afford more computation and data.

Concluding remarks

LSTMs brought a practical, powerful solution to the problem of learning long-range dependencies with RNNs. The key ideas—an additive cell state, multiplicative gates, and gated read/write semantics—provide a stable path for gradients and flexible control of information. Understanding the canonical equations, the constant error carousel intuition, and how recurrent weights mix memory gives a solid foundation for reading and evaluating research that builds on or modifies LSTM-like mechanisms. While newer architectures have challenged LSTMs in many domains, their conceptual simplicity, online capability, and strong empirical track record make them an essential tool in the sequence modeling toolkit.

Introduction

Gating mechanisms are central to modern neural sequence models. At their core, gates are learned, often nonlinear, multiplicative factors that control how information flows through a network over time and depth. They enable models to selectively retain, forget, amplify, or suppress components of signals. This selectivity is crucial for building memories that persist across many timesteps, for routing gradients during training, and for structuring computation so that different parts of a network can perform different roles. In practice, gating underlies recurrent architectures such as the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), and it is also a building block in feedforward and convolutional networks through constructs like Highway layers, Gated Linear Units (GLU), and attention-style multiplicative modulations. This chapter examines why gating is useful, the mathematics of common gate types, architectural variants that exploit gating, ways to parameterize gates, how multiple gates interact, and the practical issues that arise in training gated networks.

# Motivation for gating: selective memory, gradient control

The motivation for introducing explicit gates into neural models comes from two intertwined needs: the ability to selectively store and retrieve information over time (selective memory), and to ensure stable and useful gradient propagation during learning (gradient control). Consider a simple recurrent network with a hidden state $h_t$ updated as $h_t = \phi(Wx_t + Uh_{t-1} + b)$, where $\phi$ is a nonlinearity. When sequences are long and relevant information must be preserved across many timesteps, repeated application of $U$ and $\phi$ can either blow up or vanish the contributions from earlier inputs, making it hard for gradient-based learning to discover long-range dependencies. This is the vanishing/exploding gradient problem in the temporal domain.

Gates provide a mechanism to decouple state propagation from nonlinear transformation, allowing the network to choose when to let information pass unchanged and when to modify it. Intuitively, a gate can act as a learned valve that either lets the previous state continue forward (thereby creating memory) or replaces it with a newly computed candidate. For example, the LSTM uses a forget gate $f_t$ to rescale the previous cell state, and an input gate $i_t$ to scale the candidate update. With multiplicative scaling by values close to one or zero, the network can preserve certain components of the state precisely and avoid repeatedly applying nonlinear transformations that would attenuate or amplify those components.

From the perspective of gradients, multiplicative gates provide paths with near-linear dynamics. If a gate keeps a component of the state nearly unchanged, gradients can flow backward through many timesteps without vanishing, because the derivative of that near-identity path is close to one. Thus gates create "information highways" or "gradient highways" that stabilize learning. In addition, gates allow conditional computation: gradients are focused on parts of the model that are active for a given input, which can improve learning efficiency and capacity utilization.

Gating also supports compositionality: different gates can selectively enable different subspaces of computation, enabling the model to represent mixture-of-experts style behavior and to switch modes of processing depending on context. This conditional modulation is often more powerful and efficient than trying to encode all behaviors in a single monolithic transform.

# Sigmoid and hard-sigmoid gates, multiplicative gating

Gates are most commonly implemented using sigmoid-like functions that map a real-valued pre-activation to a gating factor in $[0,1]$. The standard continuous choice is the logistic sigmoid $\sigma(z) = 1/(1+e^{-z})$. If a gate has pre-activation $g_t = W_g x_t + U_g h_{t-1} + b_g$, then the gate value is $s_t = \sigma(g_t)$. A gate applied multiplicatively modifies an associated signal $u_t$ as $y_t = s_t \odot u_t$, where $\odot$ denotes elementwise multiplication. Because $s_t$ lies in $[0,1]$, it smoothly interpolates between blocking the signal ($s_t \approx 0$) and allowing it to pass ($s_t \approx 1$).

Sigmoid gates are differentiable everywhere and work well with gradient descent, but they have two practical characteristics to consider. First, near their extremes ($z \ll 0$ or $z \gg 0$) sigmoids saturate, with derivatives approaching zero. Saturation can slow learning if gate pre-activations move into these regions early and remain there, as gradients to the parameters controlling the gate become tiny. Second, the logistic sigmoid is relatively expensive to compute in hardware compared to simpler piecewise-linear alternatives.

A commonly used approximation is the hard-sigmoid (or hard-swish-like approximations) which is a clipped linear function: for example, $h\_sigmoid(z) = \text{clip}\left(\frac{z+1}{2}, 0, 1\right)$. This function is linear in a central band and saturates at 0 or 1 outside that band. The hard-sigmoid has nonzero constant derivative in the linear region, is computationally cheaper, and can make the network less prone to prolonged saturation if biases are initialized near the linear part. However, hard-sigmoids are only sub-differentiable at the clipping points; in practice this is not a problem with gradient-based optimization.

Multiplicative gating more generally can use other squashing functions or even discrete decisions. For discrete gating (hard on/off), one can use stochastic binary gates with Bernoulli sampling or deterministic thresholding; however these break differentiability and require approximations for training such as the straight-through estimator, REINFORCE-style policy gradients, or relaxation methods (Gumbel-Softmax). Continuous multiplicative gates remain the dominant choice because they preserve smooth gradients while still enabling selective scaling of information.

# Gated Linear Units (GLU), Highway networks, and variants

Gated Linear Units (GLU) and Highway networks are two influential gated feedforward constructs that bring the benefits of gating from recurrent models into deeper networks and convolutional architectures. The common design pattern is to split a layer's computation into a candidate transform and a gate that modulates the candidate, often with a residual connection preserved by a linear path.

A Gated Linear Unit computes an output given input $x$ as
$y = (W x + b) \odot \sigma(V x + c)$.
Here $W x + b$ is a linear transform producing the proposal, while $\sigma(V x + c)$ is the gate. Importantly, the candidate remains linear in $x$, so if the gate is set to one, the path from $x$ to $y$ is linear and gradients can flow directly. When the gate is parametrized in a way that allows it to be near-identity, GLUs can provide gradient highways through deep stacks. GLUs have been adopted in convolutional sequence models and in Transformer-like variations for their simplicity and favorable optimization properties.

Highway networks are an earlier and closely related idea designed to enable very deep feedforward networks. A highway layer produces
$y = T(x) \odot H(x) + (1 - T(x)) \odot x$,
where $H(x)$ is a nonlinear transform, and $T(x)$ is a gate (commonly sigmoid). The gate $T(x)$ interpolates between updating the representation via $H(x)$ and carrying forward the input unchanged. This equation has an explicit residual connection: if $T(x)$ is zero, the identity is preserved. Highway networks were a precursor to modern residual networks and showed how gating could permit the training of much deeper networks by offering learned skip connections.

Variants of these ideas include gated tanh or gated activation units, GLU with different nonlinearities (for instance replacing $\sigma$ with a softmax-like normalization over channels), and bidirectional gating where multiple gates interact to produce richer conditional computations. Another variant is the multiplicative integration between two pathways, e.g. combining $x$ and $h$ via elementwise multiplication after linear projection, which can be viewed as a continuous gating of one pathway by the other.

# Gate parameterization: input-dependent vs hidden-dependent gating

Gates can be parameterized to depend on different signals. The two common choices are input-dependent gating and hidden-dependent gating, and many practical architectures combine both.

Input-dependent gating computes a gate solely from the current input $x_t$: $s_t = \sigma(W_s x_t + b_s)$. This lets the model gate information based on properties of the current observation. For feedforward models and convolutional layers, input-dependent gates are natural: a GLU gate that looks only at the same receptive field information can decide, for instance, to emphasize certain features and suppress others based solely on the input patch. Input-dependent gating is modular and often cheaper to compute.

Hidden-dependent gating uses the previous hidden state $h_{t-1}$: $s_t = \sigma(U_s h_{t-1} + b_s)$. In recurrent models, hidden-dependent gates let the model decide gating behavior based on its internal memory, so the decision of whether to keep or replace parts of the state can depend on the stored context. For example, an LSTM forget gate uses both the new input and the previous hidden state to decide how much of prior cell memory to keep.

In many architectures, gates depend on both input and hidden signals: $s_t = \sigma(W_s x_t + U_s h_{t-1} + b_s)$. This combined parameterization is expressive: the gate can be triggered by features in the input when appropriate, and modulated by internal context. In feedforward deep networks, the analog is gating that depends on projections of the same input across different channels or groups.

Choosing between these parameterizations involves trade-offs. Input-only gating is simpler and may generalize better when long-term dependencies are less important. Hidden-dependent gating can be more powerful for temporal reasoning but adds parameters and computational cost, and requires careful initialization to avoid saturation. The choice also affects the gradient pathways: hidden-dependent gates create additional recurrent paths for gradients, which can either help or complicate learning.

# Interactions between multiple gates and their initialization

Many architectures employ multiple gates that interact multiplicatively. For example, the LSTM has an input gate $i_t$, forget gate $f_t$, and output gate $o_t$. The cell update is typically
$c_t = f_t \odot c_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde{c}_t$,
$h_t = o_t \odot \phi(c_t)$.
Here, interactions between $f_t$ and $i_t$ determine how much of the previous cell is preserved versus replaced by the candidate $\tilde{c}_t$. Such multiplicative interactions are powerful but can also be fragile: if both $f_t$ and $i_t$ are near zero, the cell receives little information and gradients are small; if both are near one, the cell may accumulate unbounded quantities. Careful initialization and bias terms are used to steer gate behavior in the early stages of training.

A common practical technique is bias initialization. For forget gates, initializing the bias $b_f$ to a positive value (e.g. 1 or 2) makes $f_t$ initially closer to one, encouraging the model to remember information at the start of training and providing long-range gradient paths. Conversely, initializing input gate biases negatively can prevent rapid overwriting. This heuristic has been widely adopted for LSTMs and other gated recurrent networks and often yields faster convergence and better performance.

When multiple gates are used, coupling them or constraining them can aid stability. For instance, tying $i_t = 1 - f_t$ reduces the degrees of freedom and ensures a conservation-like property between retaining and writing, reminiscent of Highway networks where $T(x)$ and $1-T(x)$ form complementary paths. While coupling reduces expressivity, it simplifies learning and can prevent degenerate regimes. Another consideration is the scale of the pre-activations: using separate parameter matrices with different initialization scales allows some gates to operate on a different dynamic range; layer normalization or batch normalization on gate preactivations can help standardize their behaviors.

Understanding the Jacobian of gated systems helps predict interactions. Because gates multiply signals, their derivatives include terms proportional to both the gate and the gated content. For example, the derivative of $f_t \odot c_{t-1}$ with respect to parameters of $f_t$ involves $c_{t-1}$, and with respect to parameters controlling $c_{t-1}$ involves $f_t$. Thus a strongly closed gate (gate near zero) can starve gradients to parameters that control the gated content. Initialization that ensures some paths are open initially helps avoid such starvation.

# Practical considerations: saturation, gradient flow, regularization

Practically deploying gated architectures requires attention to several training and generalization issues.

Saturation is a core concern. Sigmoid-like gates saturate for large magnitude inputs, yielding near-zero gradients and effectively freezing gate behavior. This can trap the model in suboptimal regimes. Several remedies exist. Bias initialization, as noted above, can position gate pre-activations in a desirable regime initially. Choosing activation functions with wider linear regions, such as hard-sigmoid, reduces the area of severe saturation. Normalization techniques—layer normalization, batch normalization (where appropriate), or weight normalization—can stabilize the statistics of pre-activations and keep them in regions where gradients are informative.

Gradient flow through multiplicative gates can be both a blessing and a curse. Gates that create near-identity paths support long-range gradient propagation, but gates that shut early can cut off gradients to large parts of the network. Monitoring gate activations during training is a useful diagnostic: if many gates saturate at 0 or 1 too quickly, learning may stall. Techniques like gradual annealing of gate biases or adding auxiliary losses to encourage gate diversity can help.

Regularization in gated networks requires special thought because gating can create brittle specialization. Dropout applied on the main activations can interact poorly with multiplicative gates: dropping the gated content while the gate is left intact can create large variance in the output. Variants such as variational dropout, zoneout, and Gal & Ghahramani-style dropout adapted for recurrent architectures replace naive dropout with structured noise that respects recurrent dynamics. Zoneout, for example, randomly preserves previous hidden units instead of replacing them with zero, which is conceptually similar to stochastic gating of the carry path and has been shown to regularize recurrent models while preserving gradient highways.

Weight decay and other parameter regularizers remain useful for gates as well. However, one must be cautious: overly strong L2 penalties on gate parameters can bias gates toward their default value (depending on bias) and reduce flexibility. Sparsity-inducing regularizers on gates can be used when one wishes to push gates toward binary behaviors for model compression or interpretability, but such regimes require careful training schedules and possibly straight-through estimators.

Finally, computational considerations matter. Hard or binary gates can reduce computation at inference by skipping operations, enabling conditional computation. But training such systems efficiently requires differentiable relaxations or reinforcement-like training, and the benefits must be weighed against increased variance in gradients and implementation complexity. For most general-purpose models, simple continuous gates (sigmoid or hard-sigmoid) with sensible initialization, normalization, and mild regularization provide a robust starting point.

Conclusion

Gating mechanisms are a powerful and flexible tool for designing neural sequence models and deep networks. They enable selective memory, improve gradient flow, and allow conditional computation. Understanding the trade-offs between continuous and discrete gates, how to parameterize gates relative to inputs and hidden states, how multiple gates interact, and how to mitigate practical problems such as saturation and poor gradient flow is crucial to both using existing gated architectures and innovating new ones. Mastery of these concepts will make it far easier to read, implement, and improve the large body of research that relies on gating to manage information dynamics in neural networks.

Introduction

Attention mechanisms and the Transformer architecture have become foundational to modern natural language processing and many other sequence modeling tasks. Unlike recurrent or convolutional architectures that process inputs with an inductive bias toward locality or temporal recurrence, attention provides a flexible, data-driven way to compute interactions between all positions in an input sequence. This capacity to model long-range dependencies efficiently (in the sense of expressiveness, not always in compute) is the reason Transformers underpin large language models, machine translation systems, and many state-of-the-art models across modalities.

This chapter explains the core ingredients of attention and the Transformer. We begin with the basic building block: scaled dot-product attention, which uses queries, keys, and values to compute contextualized representations. We then describe the softmax-based attention weights and how to interpret them. Next, we introduce multi-head attention: why multiple heads help and how they are implemented. Handle the problem that attention alone is permutation-invariant and therefore requires positional encodings to incorporate sequence order. After that, we examine the full Transformer block: how attention and feed-forward layers are combined, and why residual connections and layer normalization are important for training and stability. Finally, we analyze the computational and memory costs of self-attention, emphasizing the quadratic complexity in sequence length and implications for scaling. Throughout, we provide intuition, equations, and practical considerations you will need to read and implement research papers in the field.

# Scaled dot-product attention: queries, keys, values

Attention computes a new representation for each position in a sequence by mixing information from other positions, using content-dependent weights. A commonly used formulation is scaled dot-product attention, which relies on the concepts of queries, keys, and values. The idea is analogous to information retrieval: each position issues a "query" that asks which other positions are relevant; every position has a "key" that describes its content for matching purposes and a "value" that carries the information to be aggregated when there is a match.

Formally, suppose we have an input sequence of length $n$, and at some layer we represent each position by a vector. We linearly project these input vectors into three sets of vectors: queries $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d_k}$, keys $K \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d_k}$, and values $V \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d_v}$. Each row of $Q$ is the query for a position, each row of $K$ is the key for a position, and each row of $V$ is the value for a position. These projections are learned linear transformations: $Q = XW^Q$, $K = XW^K$, and $V = XW^V$ with $W^Q, W^K \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times d_k}$ and $W^V \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times d_v}$ when $X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d_\text{model}}$ are the input representations.

Scaled dot-product attention uses the dot product between queries and keys to measure similarity and then uses that similarity to weight values. For a single query vector $q \in \mathbb{R}^{d_k}$ and keys $K = [k_1; \dots; k_n]$, the raw compatibility scores are $q k_i^\top$. For numerical stability and to keep magnitudes well-behaved as $d_k$ grows, these scores are scaled by $\sqrt{d_k}$. The full attention output for all queries is computed as:

$${\rm Attention}(Q,K,V) = {\rm softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) V.$$

Here $\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ contains pairwise scaled dot products between every query and every key, and the softmax is applied row-wise so each query's similarity scores turn into a distribution over the keys. Multiplying by $V$ aggregates the values according to those distributions, producing an output of shape $\mathbb{R}^{n \times d_v}$.

Intuitively, scaled dot-product attention allows each position to "attend" to the most relevant positions according to learned similarity. The scaling by $\sqrt{d_k}$ is important: if $d_k$ is large, the unscaled dot products tend to have large variance, which drives the softmax into extremely peaky regions and makes gradients small; division by $\sqrt{d_k}$ controls the variance, leading to better-behaved training dynamics.

# Softmax attention, attention weights and interpretation

The softmax in the attention equation converts raw similarity scores into normalized, nonnegative weights that sum to one for each query. If we denote the unnormalized scores matrix as $S = \frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}$, then the attention weights matrix $A$ has entries

$$A_{ij} = \frac{\exp(S_{ij})}{\sum_{l=1}^n \exp(S_{il})}.$$

Each row $A_{i,:}$ is a probability distribution over positions in the sequence, giving the contribution of each key (and associated value) to the output for query $i$. This probabilistic interpretation is useful: it lets us view attention as a soft, differentiable selection mechanism where the model learns to place high mass on a few relevant positions and low mass elsewhere.

Interpreting attention weights can give insight into model behavior. For example, in language tasks, attention weights may reveal that a subject pronoun attends strongly to its antecedent several tokens earlier, or that a verb attends to its object. However, attention weights are not a definitive explanation of model behavior: they show where the model is drawing information, but downstream linear transformations, residual connections, and subsequent layers change representations further. Attention weights are a helpful diagnostic but should not be overinterpreted as causal explanations without further analysis.

From a computational perspective, the softmax operation is differentiable and supports efficient implementation on GPUs. Its row normalization ensures that the weighted sum of values is a convex combination of the value vectors, which helps maintain numerical stability. There are also variants of softmax attention: additive attention (using a small feed-forward network to compute scores), sparse or hard attention (where distributions are constrained or discretized), and temperature-controlled softmax (where a scalar temperature multiplies the logits before softmax). The standard Transformer uses scaled dot-product attention with softmax because it is simple, fast on dense hardware, and empirically effective.

# Multi-head attention: motivation and implementation

A single attention operation with $d_k$-dimensional queries and keys gives a single similarity measure per query-key pair. But different types of dependencies might be easier to capture in different representation subspaces: syntactic relations might be best represented along some dimensions, semantic coreference along others, and positional cues along yet other dimensions. Multi-head attention addresses this by running several attention "heads" in parallel, each operating on a different learned linear projection of the inputs. Each head attends to the sequence using its own queries, keys, and values, allowing the model to capture multiple types of relationships simultaneously.

Concretely, suppose we want $h$ heads. For head number $j$, we project the input $X$ with learned matrices $W_j^Q \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times d_k}$, $W_j^K \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times d_k}$, and $W_j^V \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times d_v}$. We compute

$$Q_j = X W_j^Q,\quad K_j = X W_j^K,\quad V_j = X W_j^V,$$

and then

$$\text{head}_j = \text{Attention}(Q_j,K_j,V_j) = \text{softmax}\!\left(\frac{Q_j K_j^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) V_j.$$
