Each head outputs an $n \times d_v$ matrix. We then concatenate the outputs of all heads along the feature dimension to get an $n \times (h d_v)$ matrix, and finally project back to $d_\text{model}$ dimensions with a learned matrix $W^O \in \mathbb{R}^{h d_v \times d_\text{model}}$:

$$\text{MultiHead}(Q,K,V) = \text{concat}(\text{head}_1,\dots,\text{head}_h) W^O.$$

A typical choice in the original Transformer is $d_v = d_k = d_\text{model}/h$, so that the concatenated heads match the model dimensionality. The reason for splitting into smaller subspaces is both computational and inductive: each head has lower-dimensional projections to focus on certain relationships, and the concatenation plus output projection allows the model to combine these diverse signals.

Intuitively, multi-head attention provides representational richness: different heads can learn to attend to different classes of dependencies (short-range vs long-range, syntactic vs semantic). Practically, multi-head attention improves performance compared to a single head with the same total dimensionality, because learning specialized subspaces is easier than forcing one head to express all relationships within one representation.

Implementation-wise, multi-head attention is efficient because the projections for all heads can be implemented as single large matrix multiplications followed by reshaping, which GPUs handle well. Instead of running $h$ separate small matmuls, frameworks typically compute $Q = X W^Q_\text{all}$ where $W^Q_\text{all} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_\text{model} \times (h d_k)}$ and then reshape to separate heads. This batching yields high throughput without sacrificing the logical separation of heads.

# Positional encodings and handling sequence order

A pure attention mechanism is permutation-equivariant: if you reorder the input sequence and apply the same attention computation, the outputs reorder in the same way but the model cannot distinguish sequences that are permutations of each other. In many sequence tasks, order matters—word order in language determines meaning. Therefore, Transformers must inject information about absolute or relative position into the inputs so that attention can use it.

There are two main strategies for encoding position: absolute positional encodings added to input embeddings, and relative positional encodings incorporated into the attention computation. The original Transformer paper used sinusoidal positional encodings: a deterministic function of position $p$ and dimension index $i$,

$$\text{PE}(p,2i) = \sin\!\left(\frac{p}{10000^{2i/d_\text{model}}}\right), \quad \text{PE}(p,2i+1) = \cos\!\left(\frac{p}{10000^{2i/d_\text{model}}}\right).$$

These vectors are added to token embeddings at the bottom of the model so that each position gets a unique but smoothly varying offset. The sinusoidal form has the property that relative positions can be represented as linear functions, which may help generalization to sequence lengths not seen during training.

Learned absolute positional embeddings are an alternative: a learned vector for each position up to a fixed maximum length is added to token embeddings. Learned embeddings can be more flexible and tend to perform as well or better in practice when sufficient data is available. However, they do not naturally generalize to longer sequences than the learned maximum.

Relative positional encodings encode the distance or relative order between query and key positions directly into the attention scores. One way to do this is to add a learned bias $b_{i-j}$ or a function of the distance when computing the raw score between a query at position $i$ and a key at position $j$:

$$S_{ij} = \frac{Q_i K_j^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}} + B_{i-j},$$

where $B$ is parameterized for different relative distances (possibly truncated). Relative encodings have empirical benefits in tasks where relative position patterns matter (like language with frequent local dependencies) and for models processing longer sequences. More sophisticated schemes combine content-based and relative terms in a way that keeps efficient implementation.

In summary, positional encodings break permutation symmetry by giving attention a way to prefer or discount tokens based on position. The choice between absolute learned, sinusoidal, and relative encodings depends on the task, data regime, and computational constraints. Relative encodings often give better capture of distance-sensitive patterns, while absolute encodings are simpler and widely used.

# Transformer block structure, residual connections and layer norm

A Transformer model is built by stacking multiple identical blocks (or layers). Each block contains two main sub-layers: a multi-head self-attention mechanism and a position-wise feed-forward network. The standard Transformer "encoder" block (from the original paper) is organized as follows:

1. Multi-head self-attention sub-layer.
2. Add & Layer Normalization.
3. Position-wise feed-forward sub-layer (a small two-layer MLP applied independently at each position).
4. Add & Layer Normalization.

More explicitly, if $x$ is the input to a block, the attention sub-layer computes $y = \text{MultiHead}(x,x,x)$ for self-attention. Then the block applies a residual connection and layer normalization:

$$x' = \text{LayerNorm}(x + y).$$

The feed-forward sub-layer is a two-layer MLP applied to each position independently (same parameters for each position), typically with a ReLU or GELU activation:

$$\text{FFN}(x') = W_2 \, \sigma(W_1 x' + b_1) + b_2.$$

Then another residual and normalization:

$$\text{output} = \text{LayerNorm}(x' + \text{FFN}(x')).$$

Residual connections (the additions) are crucial. They let gradients flow directly through the identity path, mitigating vanishing gradients and making it easier to train very deep stacks of layers. Intuitively, residuals let a layer make small, incremental changes to representations instead of having to produce the entire transformed representation from scratch.

Layer normalization stabilizes training by normalizing activations across the feature dimension for each position, controlling covariate shift and making the optimization landscape smoother. LayerNorm differs from batch normalization in that it normalizes per example and per position, which is important for variable-length sequences and when batch statistics are unsuitable. In practice, the order of LayerNorm and residual connections has variants; the original Transformer uses "post-norm" (LayerNorm after the addition) while a later variation called "pre-norm" applies LayerNorm before the sub-layer. Pre-norm often yields more stable training for very deep Transformers because it keeps the sub-layer input normalized.

The position-wise feed-forward network expands the representational capability of the model beyond the linear mixing that attention provides. Specifically, FFN uses a hidden dimension $d_\text{ff}$ larger than $d_\text{model}$ (e.g., 4$x$ the model size) to implement a local nonlinearity at each position. Because FFN acts independently for each position, it cannot mix information across positions; instead, its role is to transform each position's aggregated context into richer features.

Putting these components together—self-attention for cross-position mixing, FFN for local nonlinear transformation, residuals for training stability, and LayerNorm for normalization—produces a highly trainable block that can be stacked many times. The encoder stacks such blocks; the decoder stack adds masked attention and encoder–decoder attention in a similar residual+LayerNorm pattern.

# Computational and memory cost of self-attention (quadratic complexity)

Self-attention appears powerful because it allows each position to attend to every other position, but this universality has computational and memory costs. The principal bottleneck is the computation and storage of the $n \times n$ attention score matrix $S = QK^\top$ when the sequence length is $n$. Computing $QK^\top$ requires $O(n^2 d_k)$ operations (matrix multiplication cost) and storing the full score matrix requires $O(n^2)$ memory. After computing scores, applying softmax and multiplying by $V$ adds further $O(n^2 d_v)$ work and memory. Thus, both compute time and memory scale quadratically in sequence length $n$ (ignoring constants and factorization by $d_k$ or $d_v$). For long sequences, this quickly becomes prohibitive.

To be concrete, consider $d_k$ and $d_v$ are constant or scale modestly with $d_\text{model}$. Then doubling the sequence length increases compute and memory fourfold. For tasks requiring sequences of length thousands or more, or for models that must be trained or fine-tuned with very long contexts, naive self-attention becomes a practical limitation.

The quadratic cost motivates several strategies:

- Limited attention windows: Restrict attention to a local window of size $w$, so each query attends only to $w$ nearby keys. This reduces complexity to $O(n w d)$ and is effective when most dependencies are local, as in many language tasks.

- Sparse or structured attention: Design sparsity patterns (strided, block-local, dilated) so that the attention matrix has only $O(n \log n)$ or $O(n \sqrt{n})$ nonzeros. These patterns try to capture both local and some long-range interactions while avoiding full $n^2$ computation.

- Low-rank approximations: Use kernel-based methods or randomized approximations to factorize attention so that $QK^\top$ is approximated by products involving smaller intermediate matrices, reducing complexity to near-linear in $n$ under certain assumptions.

- Memory/compressed attention: Compress keys and values into a smaller set (e.g., via pooling, clustering, or learned summarization) so queries attend to fewer representatives.

- Recurrent or sliding mechanisms: Combine attention with recurrent passes or cache previously computed keys/values so that attention is computed against a limited memory rather than the entire sequence each time.

Each approach trades off exact global attention for practical scalability. The best choice depends on the task: some require precise long-range interactions (e.g., algorithmic reasoning) while others can tolerate approximate or sparse attention.

On hardware considerations, GPUs and TPUs favor dense matrix operations, so dense attention can be highly optimized and fast for moderate $n$ (hundreds to a few thousands). But memory limits on accelerators constrain the maximum sequence length for which training is feasible. Techniques such as gradient checkpointing, mixed precision, and sharding across devices mitigate but do not remove the fundamental quadratic scaling.

Understanding this tradeoff matters when reading research: claims about model capabilities must be judged against the context length used during training, and many research efforts focus on modifying attention to allow longer contexts efficiently. As sequence lengths continue to be an important frontier (e.g., long-document understanding, code, or multi-modal streams), attention complexity will remain a central engineering and research challenge.

Conclusion

Attention (queries, keys, values and the softmax weighting) is a compact, differentiable mechanism for content-based information routing. Multi-head attention multiplies the model's capacity by letting multiple subspaces model different interactions. Because attention is permutation-equivariant, positional encodings are needed to give models access to sequence order. Transformer blocks combine attention with per-position feed-forward networks, stabilized by residual connections and layer normalization, yielding a stackable architecture that is straightforward to scale. The power of self-attention comes with a cost: quadratic compute and memory in sequence length. Recognizing these costs and the design choices that address them is essential for implementing, scaling, and understanding Transformer-based models in contemporary research.

Chapter 9: Linear and Efficient Attention Approaches

Introduction

Self-attention is the central operation in modern sequence models: it computes pairwise interactions between tokens and yields strong representational power. The canonical attention mechanism used in Transformers computes attention weights from queries $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$ and keys $K \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$ via a scaled dot-product, producing an $n \times n$ dense matrix of affinities and then weighting values $V \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d_v}$. Concretely, the output for one head is
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V) = \mathrm{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\right)V,
\]
which requires $O(n^2 d)$ time and $O(n^2)$ memory to form and normalize the $n \times n$ matrix. For long sequences this quadratic cost in sequence length $n$ becomes a bottleneck in computation and memory. This chapter surveys methods that aim to reduce that cost to linear or near-linear, by approximating the attention operation while preserving useful representational behavior. We focus on two broad families: kernelized and factorized/low-rank approaches that change the algebraic structure of attention to permit linear-time implementations, and sparse/locality-biased approaches that limit the set of token-token interactions. For each method we discuss intuition, mathematics, implementation details, and practical trade-offs: how much approximation error is introduced, and what are the consequences for downstream performance.

# Kernelized attention and random feature approximations (Performer)

The key observation behind kernelized attention is that the softmax attention can be viewed as a kernel-based weighted average. The attention weights can be written elementwise as
\[
A_{ij} = \frac{\exp(q_i^\top k_j)}{\sum_{t=1}^n \exp(q_i^\top k_t)},
\]
where $q_i$ and $k_j$ are the $i$-th query and $j$-th key respectively (we omit scaling by $\sqrt{d}$ for clarity). This is a normalized kernel of the form
\[
A_{ij} = \frac{\kappa(q_i, k_j)}{\sum_{t=1}^n \kappa(q_i, k_t)},
\]
with $\kappa(x,y)=\exp(x^\top y)$. If we can express $\kappa(x,y)$ as an inner product of feature maps $\phi(x)^\top \phi(y)$, then
\[
\sum_{t=1}^n \kappa(q_i,k_t) v_t = \phi(q_i)^\top \sum_{t=1}^n \phi(k_t) v_t,
\]
so the attention output can be written without forming the $n \times n$ matrix:
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)_i = \frac{\phi(q_i)^\top \left(\sum_{t=1}^n \phi(k_t) v_t^\top\right)}{\phi(q_i)^\top \left(\sum_{t=1}^n \phi(k_t)\right)} .
\]
Because the sums over $t$ are shared across all queries, they can be precomputed in $O(n d_\phi d_v)$ time and then each query is processed in $O(d_\phi d_v)$, yielding overall linear dependency in $n$ when $d_\phi$ and $d_v$ are independent of $n$.

However, the softmax kernel $\kappa(x,y)=\exp(x^\top y)$ does not admit a finite-dimensional positive feature map $\phi$ of small size. Performer (Choromanski et al., 2021) uses random feature approximations to approximate the softmax kernel with an inner product of randomized finite-dimensional feature maps. The core idea is to find a mapping $\phi: \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^{r}_+$ such that
\[
\kappa(x,y) \approx \phi(x)^\top \phi(y),
\]
with $r$ reasonably small (e.g., hundreds or thousands) and $\phi(x) \ge 0$ elementwise to preserve positivity needed for normalization.

One way to obtain such approximations is via Random Fourier Features (for shift-invariant kernels) or via special constructions for non-shift kernels. The Performer uses a construction called FAVOR+ (Fast Attention Via positive Orthogonal Random features), which approximates $\exp(q^\top k)$ using random projections and elementwise nonlinearities. A simple sketch: draw random Gaussian matrices $W \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times d}$ and define
\[
\phi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{r}} \exp\left( \frac{\|x\|^2}{2}\right) \exp(W x),
\]
where the exponential is applied elementwise; expectations over $W$ yield $\mathbb{E}[\phi(x)^\top \phi(y)] = \exp(x^\top y)$. In practice, numerically stable variants, orthogonalized random matrices, and normalization tricks are used to reduce variance and avoid overflow. The Performer implementation yields an attention approximation called FAVOR+ attention that replaces the softmax attention with
\[
\widetilde{\mathrm{Attn}}(Q,K,V) = D(Q)^{-1} \big( \phi(Q) (\phi(K)^\top V) \big),
\]
where $\phi(Q)$ denotes applying $\phi$ rowwise to queries, and $D(Q)$ is a diagonal matrix performing per-query normalization: $D_{ii}(Q) = \phi(q_i)^\top \sum_{t}\phi(k_t)$.

There are several important practical points. First, the approximation is unbiased in expectation over the random features, and variance decreases with $r$, the number of random features. Second, the nonnegativity of $\phi$ is important to keep the normalization denominator positive and to mimic softmax behavior. Third, Performer can handle causal/auto-regressive masking by performing the sum over keys/values incrementally in a streaming fashion: the precomputed prefix sums can be used to compute causal attention outputs in $O(n)$ memory and time per layer. Fourth, Performer trades approximation error for reduced memory/computation: choosing $r$ too small increases variance and can degrade accuracy, while large $r$ approaches standard attention cost.

Despite these advantages, kernelized random-feature methods face limitations. The required number of features $r$ to reach a small approximation error may scale with sequence length or with properties of the queries/keys (e.g., their norms and angular spread). Numerical stability of exponential features must be addressed; training dynamics can differ from exact attention, and hyperparameters such as $r$, random-seed choices, and normalization require tuning.

# Linear Transformer and factorized attention methods

A different route shifts the focus from approximating the kernel to algebraically reordering the computation so attention looks like a sequence of matrix products that can be computed incrementally without $O(n^2)$ storage. Linear Transformer (Katharopoulos et al., 2020) introduced a class of “linear” attention mechanisms that replace the softmax nonlinearity with a feature map that is linear in its inputs or otherwise allows factoring.

Start from the attention formula in kernel form:
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V) = D(Q)^{-1} \left(\Phi(Q) \left(\Phi(K)^\top V\right)\right),
\]
where $\Phi$ applies some elementwise map to each row (the kernel feature map). If $\Phi$ is chosen such that $\Phi(x) = \psi(x)$ with relatively small dimension and simple computations, the product $\Phi(K)^\top V$ can be computed in $O(n d_\phi d_v)$, and each query can be handled in $O(d_\phi d_v)$.

Katharopoulos et al. proposed using a simple positive map like $\psi(x) = \mathrm{elu}(x) + 1$ (where $\mathrm{elu}$ is the exponential linear unit), which is positive for all inputs and empirically behaved similarly to softmax for many tasks. The resulting attention is called Linear Transformer. The key computational advantage is that attention can be computed as a sequence of matrix products that avoid forming the $n \times n$ matrix, and causal attention can be computed in a streaming left-to-right manner by maintaining appropriate running sums:
\[
S_t = \sum_{u=1}^t \psi(k_u) v_u^\top, \qquad Z_t = \sum_{u=1}^t \psi(k_u),
\]
and then the output for position $t$ is
\[
o_t = \frac{\psi(q_t)^\top S_t}{\psi(q_t)^\top Z_t}.
\]
This enables $O(n)$ memory and $O(n)$ per layer time (ignoring constants) and supports autoregressive generation with constant incremental cost per token.

Factorized attention methods are more general: they search for factorizations of the $n \times n$ attention matrix into lower-dimensional factors or products of sparse matrices. One line of work approximates attention by decomposing it as a product $A \approx L R^\top$ with $L, R \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times r}$ and $r \ll n$, or as a sequence of structured multiplications that share computations. Another approach is to apply separable convolutions or Toeplitz-like structures to model local interactions efficiently. The linear transformer family emphasizes reengineering the nonlinearity to permit such algebraic factoring with small feature dimension $d_\phi$.

Intuitively, factorized attention methods succeed when the effective kernel space required to represent query-key interactions is low-dimensional or when the nonlinearity can be approximated by low-dimensional features. The simplicity of maps like $\mathrm{elu}+1$ also helps numerical stability compared to exponential random feature formulations, though there is no exact equivalence to softmax attention: the change in nonlinearity can alter the model’s inductive biases and optimization landscape. Empirically, linear transformers have shown competitive performance in tasks emphasizing streaming or very long sequences, but for many tasks with medium-length sequences, exact softmax attention still yields better performance unless careful tuning or task-specific augmentation is applied.

# Linformer and low-rank attention approximations

Linformer (Wang et al., 2020) takes a different perspective: it hypothesizes that the $n \times n$ attention matrix often has low intrinsic rank, so attention can be approximated by projecting keys and values into a lower-dimensional sequence length dimension before computing pairwise interactions. Concretely, instead of computing attention weights between all $n$ queries and $n$ keys, Linformer applies learnable linear projections $E_K, E_V \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}$ (or equivalent row-wise projections) to reduce the sequence dimension:
\[
\widehat{K} = E_K^\top K, \qquad \widehat{V} = E_V^\top V,
\]
where $\widehat{K}, \widehat{V} \in \mathbb{R}^{k \times d}$ with $k \ll n$. Attention is then computed as
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,\widehat{K},\widehat{V}) = \mathrm{softmax}\!\left(\frac{Q \widehat{K}^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\widehat{V}.
\]
Because $\widehat{K}$ and $\widehat{V}$ have only $k$ rows, the affinity matrix becomes $n \times k$ rather than $n \times n$. The time complexity becomes $O(n k d)$ and memory $O(n k)$, so if $k$ can stay constant or grow slowly with $n$, this is nearly linear.

The Linformer argument is supported by empirical observations and theoretical heuristics: in many natural signals, attention maps have low rank because each query attends only to a low-dimensional subspace of the key space, or the attention patterns can be well-approximated by a basis of size $k$. Linformer parameterizes the projection matrices and learns them end-to-end, allowing the model to discover the subspace most useful for attention. A practical implementation uses shared projection matrices across heads or layers to limit parameters.

There are important caveats. First, low-rank approximations cannot represent attention patterns that are inherently high-rank, such as near-orthogonal attention weights or patterns that require fine-grained per-position distinctions. Second, Linformer’s projection matrices are typically full-row projections across sequence positions, which limits flexibility to model arbitrary position-relative interactions; some versions add positional-specific parameters or make projections position-dependent. Third, choosing $k$ trades off between accuracy and efficiency: too small a $k$ causes representational bottlenecks; too large erodes the computational savings.

Mathematically, Linformer approximates the original matrix $A = \mathrm{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\right)$ by $A \approx B C$ where $B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}$ and $C \in \mathbb{R}^{k \times n}$. If the singular values of $A$ decay rapidly, a small $k$ suffices. In practice, Linformer often performs well on tasks where attention patterns are smooth or local, but it can struggle on tasks requiring precise long-range interactions unless $k$ is large.

# Sparse attention and locality-biased mechanisms

Sparse attention restricts the set of allowed pairwise interactions so the attention matrix is sparse rather than dense. The restriction can be deterministic (fixed pattern) or learned/adaptive. Sparse patterns reduce memory and computation when each row of the attention matrix has only $s \ll n$ nonzeros; then costs are $O(n s d)$ and memory $O(n s)$. Sparse attention strategies frequently exploit locality: tokens are most likely to attend to nearby tokens, so only a local window is retained. This idea underlies architectures like the Transformer-XL local attention, Longformer (Beltagy et al., 2020), and its sliding-window attention, where each position attends to a fixed window of neighbors and some positions have global attention.

Longformer combines three attention types: local sliding windows, global (learned) tokens that attend to all positions, and dilated or strided attention patterns. BigBird (Zaheer et al., 2020) builds on similar ideas by combining sparse random attention, local sliding windows, and global tokens, and proves that when combined the scheme is a universal approximator of dense attention under certain connectivity conditions. These hybrid sparse patterns aim to capture both locality and occasional global interactions with sparse structures that require only linear memory and time.

Another class of sparse approaches learns sparse attention patterns, either by parameterizing a distribution over sparsity patterns or by using top-k selection on affinity scores (sparsifying by selecting the highest k keys per query). Top-k sparsification maintains the most relevant interactions at each query but introduces non-differentiability or requires approximate differentiable top-k methods. Learned sparsity can adapt better to task structure but may demand careful optimization and regularization to avoid pathological collapse (e.g., attending to very few constant positions).

Locality-biased mechanisms exploit the observation that many sequence tasks have strong local dependencies. Convolutional or local window attention trades expressivity for efficiency but often performs very well on tasks like speech recognition or language modeling where nearby tokens are highly relevant. Multi-scale sparse patterns and hierarchical attention combine local dense attention within blocks and sparse cross-block attention, giving a compromise between full global modeling and tractable computation.

Sparse methods introduce several implementation considerations: indexing sparsity efficiently on hardware, batching with variable sparsity patterns, and ensuring efficient memory access patterns. Some sparse patterns map well to GPUs (fixed regular patterns like sliding windows), while irregular learned sparsity may be slower due to scatter/gather overheads.

# Practical trade-offs: approximation error vs efficiency

All linear or sparse attention approximations trade accuracy for computational savings. Understanding and managing approximation error is therefore crucial in practical deployments. There are multiple dimensions to this trade-off: the asymptotic cost (theoretical scaling with sequence length), the constant factors (which often dominate on realistic hardware), the introduced bias or variance of the approximation, training dynamics, and downstream task sensitivity.

Kernelized random-feature methods (Performer) introduce stochastic approximation error: approximate kernels yield unbiased estimates (in expectation) but with finite variance that depends on the number of features $r$ and the distribution of queries and keys. Increasing $r$ reduces variance but raises computation and memory. Moreover, the error can be data dependent: if queries and keys have large norms or are concentrated in a small angular sector, the variance of the random-feature approximation can be worse. In practice, one must choose $r$ large enough to make the approximation noise negligible, or otherwise incorporate variance reduction methods (orthogonal features, careful normalization). On the plus side, Performers preserve model behavior more closely to softmax attention than some other approximations when $r$ is sufficiently large.

Linear Transformers change the nonlinearity itself (e.g., replacing softmax with $\mathrm{elu}+1$), which introduces a deterministic approximation error: the attention mechanism is different and may encode different inductive biases. This can be robust in streaming/autoregressive settings and yields exact linear-time algorithms for causal decoding, but it sometimes underperforms exact softmax in tasks where precise normalization and probability-like attention weights matter. The benefit is predictable, deterministic speed and memory behavior.

Low-rank methods like Linformer reduce representation by a learned projection; their approximation error depends on how well the attention maps lie in a low-dimensional subspace. In tasks with intrinsic low-rank attention maps, Linformer provides large savings at little cost. But for tasks requiring high-rank attention (e.g., complex alignment tasks, tasks with diverse long-range dependencies), low-rank bottlenecks can markedly degrade performance. Choosing the projection dimension $k$ balances this trade-off.

Sparse methods that restrict attention patterns can be extremely efficient and, for many tasks, introduce minimal error because local and structured sparsity matches the task's dependency patterns. However, fixed sparsity patterns risk missing important global interactions and require careful design (e.g., occasional global tokens, dilated patterns) or task-specific tuning.

Beyond raw approximation error, other practical trade-offs include:

- Numerical stability: kernel approximations involving exponentials or very small denominators can cause instability; normalization schemes and positive feature maps help.
- Hardware efficiency: theoretical asymptotic savings may not translate to speedups if constant factors or memory access patterns are unfavorable. Dense matrix multiplies are heavily optimized on GPUs, so methods replacing dense $n \times n$ operations with many small or irregular operations can be slower in wall-clock time.
- Training dynamics: approximations can change gradient magnitudes and loss landscapes, sometimes requiring learning-rate adjustments or other optimizer changes.
- Flexibility: some approximations allow causal streaming and incremental decoding more naturally (linearized kernels, prefix-sum strategies), which is important for autoregressive generation.

In practice, one picks a method based on the task demands (sequence length, need for streaming, sensitivity to exact attention), available hardware, and acceptable accuracy drop. It is common to evaluate a small set of candidates (e.g., Performer with multiple $r$ values, a Linear Transformer variant, and a sparse pattern) and choose the best trade-off empirically.

# Comparisons of asymptotic cost and empirical performance

Asymptotic cost comparisons are a helpful starting point but not the whole story. We summarize the typical complexities and then comment on empirical behaviors.

- Exact softmax attention: Time $O(n^2 d)$, Memory $O(n^2)$ for the affinity matrix. This remains the gold standard for accuracy when $n$ is modest.
- Kernelized random-feature attention (Performer): Time $O(n r d + n d d_v)$ often simplified to $O(n r d)$ for feature computations; Memory $O(n r)$ for storing features. If $r$ is constant w.r.t. $n$, scaling is linear in $n$. However, $r$ may need to grow with desired precision.
- Linear Transformer (factorized kernel): Time $O(n d d_\phi)$ with $d_\phi$ usually equal to attention dimension $d$ or a small multiple; Memory $O(n d_\phi)$. Asymptotically linear in $n$.
- Linformer (low-rank): Time $O(n k d)$ and Memory $O(n k)$. If $k$ is independent of $n$, cost is linear.
- Sparse attention (sliding windows, BigBird, Longformer): Time $O(n s d)$, Memory $O(n s)$ where $s$ is the sparsity per row (window size plus global tokens). For constant $s$, linear scaling.
- Hybrid or hierarchical schemes: Complexity depends on block size and number of cross-block connections; typically near-linear with moderate constants.

Empirically, the picture is nuanced. On many long-context language modeling tasks, Performer and sparse methods (Longformer/BigBird) have shown strong performance with significant memory savings. Performers often approach the accuracy of full attention when $r$ is sufficiently large, but on some benchmarks small $r$ leads to noticeable accuracy gaps. Linear Transformers perform very well in streaming autoregressive generation tasks because of their exact causal streaming implementation, and they are very efficient for inference. Linformer can match performance on tasks where attention is effectively low-rank but can degrade when fine-grained position-dependent attention is needed.

Sparse models like Longformer and BigBird are particularly effective when locality matters or when a few global tokens suffice to relay global context. BigBird’s theoretical guarantees that certain sparse patterns can approximate dense attention under conditions suggest robust empirical performance across tasks. However, sparse models must be carefully engineered to map efficiently onto current hardware: sliding window attention can be implemented using efficient block-sparse matrix multiplication custom kernels or hand-optimized indexing, but irregular sparsity slows things down.

Benchmarks also reveal that constant factors dominate: a method with asymptotic $O(n)$ complexity but a large per-token constant (large $r$ in Performer, or large $d_\phi$ in Linear Transformer) may be slower than optimized dense attention for moderate $n$. In many practical regimes (sequence lengths of a few thousand), optimized dense attention using mixed precision and memory-efficient attention kernels can outperform poorly implemented approximations. The crossover point where linear methods become advantageous depends on hardware, batch size, and implementation.

Finally, training stability and convergence matter. Some approximations require careful initialization or optimizer settings. For instance, training with approximate attention may require gradient clipping or schedule tuning to avoid divergence. Transfer learning scenarios where pretrained dense-attention models are fine-tuned to long contexts may benefit from warm-starting or mixing approximations with full attention in early epochs.

Conclusion and practical recommendations

Linear and efficient attention approaches furnish a toolkit for scaling attention to long sequences, streaming workloads, and memory-constrained regimes. Kernelized random-feature methods (Performer) provide a principled approximation of softmax attention with tunable stochastic error and support causal streaming. Linear Transformer and related factorized models give deterministic linear-time attention by changing the feature map, often enabling efficient autoregressive decoding. Linformer and other low-rank methods exploit low intrinsic rank in attention maps to achieve large reductions when that assumption holds. Sparse and locality-biased mechanisms exploit structure in the data—locality and occasional global tokens—to obtain near-linear costs in practice.

When choosing among these methods, consider the following guidelines. If reproducing softmax behavior with bounded approximation error is important and you can afford a few hundred to a few thousand random features, Performer is a strong choice. If you need streaming autoregressive decoding with the absolute lowest incremental cost, linearized feature maps like those in Linear Transformer are attractive. If you believe attention maps are low-rank for your task or can be learned, Linformer can be very efficient. If your domain exhibits strong locality (text, audio) and only occasional global context is needed, sparse sliding-window approaches (Longformer, BigBird) are often the best practical tradeoff. In all cases, measure wall-clock performance, memory consumption, and task metrics on representative hardware and tune approximation hyperparameters ($r$, $k$, window size, sparsity pattern) carefully.

Understanding the algebraic structure behind these approximations—kernel factorization, low-rank projection, and sparsity patterns—helps reason about when they will work and how to combine them. Hybrid designs that mix sparse and low-rank components or combine random-feature kernels with block-sparse patterns often capture strengths of multiple approaches and are an active area of research. As hardware and kernels continue to evolve, these families of methods will remain important tools in scaling attention models to new applications.

Chapter 10: State-Space Models for Sequence Modeling

Introduction

State-space models (SSMs) provide a powerful framework for modeling time series and sequential data. They originated in control theory and signal processing as a way to represent dynamical systems with latent states that evolve over time and produce observations. More recently, linear SSMs with modern neural parameterizations have emerged as competitive architectures for long-range sequence modeling in machine learning, offering an alternative to recurrent neural networks and Transformers. This chapter introduces discrete-time linear SSMs, explains how they can be interpreted as sequence filters or convolutions, surveys key parameterizations used in recent models (S4, DSS, S5 and relatives), and describes connections to convolutional and filtering views. We analyze advantages and limitations, including computational complexity and expressivity, and conclude with practical training and implementation strategies such as diagonalization and FFT-based approaches.

# Discrete-time linear state-space equations

At the core of linear state-space modeling are two linear equations: one that governs the evolution of a hidden state and one that maps the hidden state to outputs. In discrete time, for time step $n = 0,1,2,\dots$, a standard linear time-invariant state-space model is written as
\[
x_{n+1} = A x_n + B u_n,
\]
\[
y_n = C x_n + D u_n.
\]
Here $x_n \in \mathbb{R}^N$ is the latent state vector at time $n$, $u_n \in \mathbb{R}^d$ is the input (or control) at time $n$, $y_n \in \mathbb{R}^m$ is the output, and $A \in \mathbb{R}^{N\times N}$, $B \in \mathbb{R}^{N\times d}$, $C \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times N}$, $D \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times d}$ are constant coefficient matrices. This is the discrete-time analogue of the continuous-time linear system given by differential equations, and it suffices for digital sequence modeling.

The first equation, called the state update or transition equation, specifies how the latent state evolves under the effect of the previous state and the current input. The second equation, often called the observation or readout equation, produces an observable output from the latent state and possibly the input. If $D\neq 0$ the model can create an instantaneous mapping from input to output; often one considers $D=0$ for simplicity and interpretability.

One important property is linear time-invariance (LTI): the matrices $A,B,C,D$ do not depend on the time index $n$. LTI systems are fully characterized by their impulse response, which describes the output sequence produced in response to an input impulse. The impulse response is central to understanding the filtering interpretation of SSMs; we will derive it explicitly below.

To make the model causal and well-posed, we also require an initial state $x_0$ (commonly set to zero). Unrolling the state update yields an explicit expression for $x_n$ in terms of past inputs:
\[
x_n = A^n x_0 + \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} A^{n-1-k} B u_k.
\]
Assuming $x_0 = 0$, the output becomes
\[
y_n = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} C A^{n-1-k} B u_k + D u_n.
\]
This shows the output at time $n$ is a linear combination of past inputs weighted by the matrices $C A^{n-1-k} B$, plus the instantaneous term $D u_n$.

The matrix powers $A^t$ thus determine how past inputs influence current outputs. Understanding and manipulating $A$ is therefore crucial: its eigenvalues control memory and stability, and its structure affects computational complexity.

# Interpretation of SSMs as sequence filters or convolutions

The unrolled expression for the output demonstrates that linear SSMs act as causal linear filters on the input sequence. For scalar inputs and outputs ($d=m=1$) and $D=0$, the output simplifies to
\[
y_n = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} h_{n-1-k} u_k,
\]
where the impulse response sequence is defined by
\[
h_t = C A^t B, \qquad t = 0,1,2,\dots.
\]
This is exactly a causal convolution of the input sequence $\{u_k\}$ with the kernel $\{h_t\}$. More generally, for vector-valued inputs and outputs the model defines a matrix-valued impulse response, but the convolutional interpretation remains: the SSM computes $y = h * u$ where $*$ denotes causal convolution.

Viewing SSMs as filters is valuable for intuition. The latent state $x_n$ provides a compact internal summary of all prior inputs; the matrices $A$ and $B$ determine how inputs are folded into this summary, while $C$ reads out a weighted sum of state coordinates to produce the output. The kernel $h_t$ captures exactly how an input $u_{n-t}$ affects $y_n$. Long-range dependencies correspond to kernels with significant mass for large $t$.

This filter viewpoint connects SSMs to classical signal-processing concepts such as impulse responses, frequency responses, and z-transforms. The discrete-time transfer function of the LTI system is
\[
H(z) = C (zI - A)^{-1} B + D,
\]
where $z$ is a complex variable. Expanding $H(z)$ as a power series in $z^{-1}$ yields the impulse response coefficients $h_t$. Frequency-domain analysis studies how different temporal frequencies in the input are attenuated or amplified by the system, guiding design choices for memory and smoothing properties.

The convolutional perspective is also useful computationally: if one can compute the kernel $h$ efficiently, then sequence outputs can be obtained via convolution. For very long sequences, convolution can be accelerated in the frequency domain using the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT), leading to sub-quadratic or even near-linear computational complexity in sequence length.

# Parameterizations used in S4, DSS, S5 and related models

Recent neural architectures for long-range sequence modeling parameterize linear SSMs in specific ways to balance efficiency, stability, and expressivity. We'll cover the ideas behind S4, DSS, and S5, highlighting common themes: structured $A$, continuous-time-to-discrete-time mapping, and parameter sharing with neural gating or projection layers.

S4 (Structured State Space Sequence model) introduced a parameterization designed to produce long, expressive convolution kernels while allowing fast parallel computation. The approach begins with a continuous-time SSM with matrix $\tilde{A}$ in companion form derived from a linear differential equation. More concretely, S4 uses the HiPPO (High-order Polynomial Projection Operators) framework to set $\tilde{A}$ so that the state maintains a compressed summary of the input function in a basis of orthogonal polynomials. These continuous-time matrices are then discretized (via bilinear transform or other discretization methods) to obtain discrete-time $A$ and $B$. To keep computations efficient, S4 leverages a particular normal plus low-rank (NPLR) decomposition: $A$ is represented as a diagonal matrix plus a low-rank perturbation. This structure permits fast computation of terms like $C A^t B$ and allows diagonalization or efficient resolvent evaluations.

S4 parameterizes the readout $C$ and input projection $B$ with learned vectors (or small matrices) and may include gating and elementwise nonlinearities around the linear SSM core. Practically, S4 transforms inputs through input and output projection layers and processes a window of inputs by convolving with the kernel derived from the SSM. The end effect is a layer that behaves like a convolution with a long learned kernel but computed efficiently via the SSM structure.

DSS (Diagonal-State Space) models emphasize parameterizing $A$ to be diagonal or nearly diagonal in some basis. A diagonal $A$ means each state coordinate evolves independently, which simplifies powers $A^t$ to elementwise exponentiation and makes computing the impulse response trivial: $h_t = \sum_{i} C_i (A_{ii})^t B_i$. Diagonal models can be very scalable and stable, and they can be augmented with learned complex-valued diagonal entries to capture oscillatory behavior. However, purely diagonal $A$ may lack interaction between state coordinates, limiting expressivity; DSS remedies this by allowing low-rank coupling or mixing layers that project inputs into the diagonal dynamics and project states back to outputs.

S5 (Simplified S4 and Smooth State Space) can be viewed as a practical simplification that retains the benefits of S4 while streamlining training and implementation. S5 uses a parameterization that starts from continuous-time models and applies an efficient discretization scheme. It typically uses diagonal-plus-low-rank $A$ matrices and explicitly constructs kernels in the frequency domain for convolution. The S5 family focuses on numerical stability, closed-form expressions for the convolution kernel (or its frequency response), and controllable memory via eigenvalue placement.

Common elements across these models include: working in continuous time for principled long-range memory, discretizing to obtain practical discrete-time matrices, imposing structured parameterizations (diagonal, diagonal-plus-low-rank, or companion forms) to permit efficient computation, and combining the linear SSM layer with elementwise nonlinearities or gating to provide greater expressivity in neural contexts. They also often use complex-valued parameters to naturally represent decaying oscillations and then implement real-valued operations via pairing complex conjugates.

# Connections between SSMs and convolutional/filtering views

The equivalence between LTI state-space models and convolutional filters makes SSM layers an instance of convolutional sequence processing with a particular parametrically defined kernel. This connection helps to build bridges between SSM-based architectures and other modern sequence models, and it suggests several algorithmic strategies.

First, any stable discrete-time LTI SSM defines an impulse response kernel $h_t = C A^t B$, and its action on an input sequence is convolution: $y = h * u$. Thus SSM layers can be implemented either by unrolling the state recurrence (sequentially or with scan-like parallel prefixes) or by computing the kernel $h$ and performing convolution. When the kernel can be computed for the entire desired length efficiently, convolution via FFT or overlap-add methods yields fast parallel evaluation, making SSM layers viable for very long sequences.

Second, SSMs can be interpreted as parameterized convolutions where the kernel is not a free tensor but is constrained by the low-dimensional matrices $A,B,C,D$. This constraint reduces memory and computation (because $A$ often has structure) and imposes certain inductive biases: smoothness, decay, or oscillation depending on eigenvalues and discretization. In contrast, pure convolutional networks typically parameterize kernels directly; SSM parameterizations aim to get long effective receptive fields without quadratic cost in sequence length or kernel size.

Third, frequency-domain reasoning is natural. The transfer function $H(e^{i\omega})$ describes the SSM filter in frequency space. Multiplying in frequency space corresponds to convolution in time, so FFT-based convolution computes $y$ by transforming input and kernel, pointwise multiplying, and inverse transforming. For models with parameterized $H(z)$, one can directly parameterize frequency responses or compute the frequency-domain kernel from $A,B,C$ formulas using rational functions. Some SSM approaches exploit this to compute convolution outputs directly in frequency domain, sometimes leading to better numerical stability or easier regularization.

Finally, there are links to dilated and causal convolutions used in deep learning, such as WaveNet-style architectures. An SSM can emulate many of those behaviors depending on the eigenstructure of $A$. Conversely, convolutional architectures with exponentially increasing dilation can approximate certain SSM impulse responses. The difference is that SSMs provide a compact global parameterization that can represent very long-range dependencies with fewer parameters, whereas dilated convolutions typically require stacking many layers or large kernels.

# Advantages and limitations (linear complexity, expressivity)

Advantages:

One major advantage of structured SSMs is the potential for linear or near-linear complexity in sequence length. If the kernel $h$ is precomputed or can be computed efficiently, then convolution via FFT gives complexity $O(L \log L)$ for sequence length $L$, which is much better than naive $O(L^2)$ attention. When $A$ is diagonal or diagonal-plus-low-rank, computing $h_t$ for all $t$ up to $L$ can be done in $O(NL)$ or even $O((N+k)\log L)$ per sequence depending on implementation, where $N$ is state dimension and $k$ is rank. This makes SSM layers attractive for long-context tasks.

SSMs also provide strong inductive biases for sequence modeling. The eigenvalues of $A$ control memory decay and oscillations, so one can design or regularize them to capture slowly decaying dependencies or periodicities. Continuous-time origin of many SSM parameterizations (HiPPO, etc.) gives mathematical guarantees about the ability to compress function spaces into finite-dimensional states, which underlies strong empirical performance on long-range tasks.

Another practical advantage is parameter efficiency: a small set of matrices $A,B,C$ can induce a very long effective kernel, often outperforming feedforward or convolutional layers with comparable parameter counts on long-context benchmarks.

Limitations:

Expressivity: Linear SSMs, by themselves, are linear operators. Modeling nonlinear phenomena requires stacking SSM layers with nonlinearities or combining SSMs with gating mechanisms. While stacking can recover powerful representations (like in S4 where SSM is embedded inside a residual block with nonlinearities), the core linear kernel is constrained by the spectral properties of $A$. Diagonal or low-rank structures further restrict the set of possible kernels. There exist kernels or temporal dependencies that require richer (e.g., fully dense) state transitions to represent compactly.

Stability and numerical issues: Ensuring stability (i.e., eigenvalues of $A$ lie within the unit circle after discretization) and managing numerical precision for long sequences can be challenging. Discretization from continuous time must be handled carefully to avoid exploding or vanishing dynamics. Complex eigenvalues lead to oscillatory kernels that must be paired with their conjugates to obtain real-valued outputs; implementations must ensure these pairings are respected numerically.

Computational trade-offs: While convolution via FFT yields $O(L \log L)$ time for a single convolution, precomputing kernels or computing transfer functions can be costly for large state dimension $N$ if done naively. Some SSM parameterizations (e.g., companion matrices) can require $O(N^3)$ work to diagonalize if generic; the use of structured matrices is essential to avoid cubic costs. Also, the constant factors in FFT-based methods and the overhead of moving between time and frequency domains can be significant in practice; for shorter sequences, direct methods or parallel scans might be faster.

Learning dynamics: Training deep stacks of SSM layers can encounter optimization difficulties unless appropriate parameterizations and initialization schemes are used. Some parameterizations incorporate explicit stability constraints or reparameterizations that tame gradients; others rely on careful initializations inspired by HiPPO matrices to bias dynamics toward useful regimes. Regularization and normalization can be important.

# Training and implementation details (diagonalization, FFT-based approaches)

Efficient and stable implementation of SSM-based layers is an engineering and algorithmic challenge. Two broad strategies are commonly used for evaluating an SSM layer on a sequence: time-domain recurrence (scan) and kernel-based convolution (with FFT or overlap-add). Each has trade-offs for parallelism, memory, and speed.

Time-domain recurrence and parallel scan: One can compute the state sequence {x_n} and outputs {y_n} by iterating the recurrence. This is inherently sequential if done naively, costing $O(N^2 L)$ for dense $A$. However, one can exploit matrix exponentials or parallel-scan algorithms. A useful trick for LTI systems is to compute powers of $A$ using binary exponentiation or exponentiation by squaring in a divide-and-conquer parallel scheme; another is to use linear recurrence exploitation such as the "linear recurrence as convolution" viewpoint which reduces the problem to convolution with precomputed coefficients. For diagonal or diagonal-plus-low-rank $A$ the recurrence becomes elementwise or nearly elementwise and can be parallelized heavily on GPUs.

Diagonalization: The central algebraic tool is diagonalizing (or more generally, bringing to Jordan or Schur form) the matrix $A$. If $A$ is diagonalizable as $A = V \Lambda V^{-1}$ with $\Lambda$ diagonal, then powers are easy: $A^t = V \Lambda^t V^{-1}$. Computing $h_t = C A^t B$ becomes $h_t = (C V) \Lambda^t (V^{-1} B)$, which reduces to weighted sums of scalar exponentials, and computing $h$ for all $t$ is cheap. The problem is that diagonalization for a dense arbitrary $A$ costs $O(N^3)$ and can be numerically unstable. To avoid this, modern SSM parameterizations choose $A$ with special structure that admits cheap diagonalization or even direct diagonal forms: diagonal matrices, Cauchy-like matrices, or diagonal-plus-low-rank forms where the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula enables efficient resolvent computations.

FFT-based convolution: Once the kernel $h$ for length $L$ is available (or can be computed in $O(NL)$ or better), convolution of $h$ with the input $u$ can be accelerated using FFTs. Zero-padding the sequences and using FFT-based multiplication yields complexity $O(L \log L)$ per channel for large $L$, with typical practical efficiencies when $L$ exceeds a few thousand. When one must process multiple sequences or batch items, batched FFTs are efficient. Overlap-add or overlap-save techniques enable streaming convolution of very long sequences in blocks.

Computing kernels via transfer functions: Instead of computing $h_t$ by repeatedly multiplying $A$, one can compute the transfer function $H(z) = C (zI - A)^{-1} B + D$ and then obtain the time-domain kernel by applying an inverse z-transform, which is effectively an inverse discrete-time Fourier transform if evaluated on the unit circle. For structured $A$, evaluating $(e^{i\omega}I - A)^{-1} B$ for many frequencies $\omega$ can be done efficiently using the Sherman-Morrison formula if $A$ is diagonal-plus-low-rank. This yields an $O(N + L \log L)$ overall strategy: evaluate $H(e^{i\omega_k})$ at FFT frequency bins $k$, multiply by the FFT of $u$, and inverse FFT back to time domain. This approach is used in S4/S5 implementations: parameterize $A$ as diagonal plus low rank, evaluate resolvent on frequency grid cheaply, get frequency-domain kernel, and perform convolution via FFT.

Handling complex-valued dynamics: Many SSMs are naturally represented with complex eigenvalues to produce oscillations. Implementations typically store complex diagonal entries in conjugate pairs and use real arithmetic by stacking real and imaginary parts or by implementing complex FFTs. Careful pairing ensures real outputs and reduces redundancy.

Stability and discretization: Continuous-time SSMs are often discretized using the bilinear transform (Tustin), zero-order hold, or exact matrix exponential to obtain discrete-time $A$ from continuous-time $\tilde{A}$. The bilinear transform maps the left-half complex plane to the unit disk, preserving stability in many cases. Exact discretization via matrix exponential is possible: given continuous-time $dx/dt = \tilde{A} x + \tilde{B} u(t)$ sampled with zero-order-hold at step $\Delta$, the discrete matrices are
\[
A = \exp(\tilde{A} \Delta),
\qquad
B = \left(\int_{0}^{\Delta} \exp(\tilde{A} s) ds\right) \tilde{B}.
\]
Computing matrix exponentials can be expensive for dense matrices, but for structured $\tilde{A}$ or diagonal $\tilde{A}$ this is cheap. Many SSM parameterizations simplify continuous-time matrices to allow efficient exact or approximate discretization.

Implementation tips and optimizations: For practical training, batching, GPU memory, and numerical stability are central concerns. Precomputing kernels for all layers when sequence length is known can amortize overhead. Mixed-precision training with care around stability-sensitive operations (e.g., using float32 for diagonalization/FFT kernels) helps. Regularization techniques such as spectral norm penalties on $A$ eigenvalues or explicit clipping can prevent exploding dynamics. Also, using residual connections and normalization (layer norm) around SSM layers improves gradient flow in deep stacks.

Finally, modern libraries implement many of these techniques: efficient low-level routines for diagonal-plus-low-rank resolvents, batched FFTs, and stable discretization. Understanding the algebraic structure of the chosen parameterization is key to implementing these models efficiently rather than relying on naive dense linear algebra.

Conclusion

Linear state-space models bridge classical signal processing and modern deep learning for sequence modeling. By interpreting SSMs as parameterized convolutional filters, one can leverage frequency-domain algorithms and structured linear algebra to achieve long-range receptive fields with favorable computational scaling. Models such as S4, DSS, and S5 illustrate how careful parameterization—diagonalization-friendly matrices, diagonal-plus-low-rank structure, and continuous-to-discrete design—yields practical, expressive layers suitable for long-context tasks. The trade-offs involve balancing expressivity and structure, ensuring numerical stability, and selecting efficient implementations (recurrence vs. convolution via FFT). With these tools and insights, SSM-based architectures are a valuable part of the sequence-modeling toolkit and a fruitful area for ongoing research.

Introduction

Associative memory is the study of systems that store and later retrieve patterns by content, rather than by explicit address. When presented with a partial, noisy, or related cue, an associative memory should produce the stored pattern that is most strongly associated with the cue. Associative memories play a central role in neuroscience and in early neural network theory, and they continue to influence modern architectures through ideas such as outer-product updates, fast weights, and attention mechanisms. This chapter traces the conceptual and mathematical development from the original Hebbian idea through classical associative models such as Hopfield networks and Bidirectional Associative Memories (BAM), to outer-product (covariance) update rules, the family of fast-weight programmers, and modern instantiations where attention and retrieval dynamics realize associative memory functionality. Along the way we analyze capacity limits, the nature of interference (crosstalk), normalization strategies that mitigate interference, and how modern soft attention can be seen as a probabilistic, normalized form of outer-product associative storage.

# Hebbian learning and basic associative memory ideas

Hebbian learning is the oldest and simplest synaptic plasticity idea: "neurons that fire together, wire together." Formally, for two units (or populations) with activations $x$ and $y$, a Hebbian synaptic update has the simple outer-product form
\[
\Delta W \propto x\, y^\top.
\]
This rule strengthens connections between co-active pre- and postsynaptic units. The rule is local (it uses only pre- and postsynaptic activities) and unsupervised. In the simplest case, with $y=x$ (associating a pattern with itself), Hebbian learning stores correlations between components of a single pattern into a weight matrix $W$. When many patterns are stored by accumulating outer products,
\[
W = \sum_{\mu=1}^P x^\mu {x^\mu}^\top,
\]
the resulting matrix encodes pairwise associations between coordinates: if two components frequently co-occur across stored patterns, the corresponding weight will be large.

Intuitively, Hebbian learning creates "attractors" in activity space. When the network dynamics later run with the weight matrix $W$, inputs that are similar to a stored pattern are driven toward it, because the parts of the input that match a stored pattern receive reinforcement from their co-active partners via $W$. This is the central associative idea: storage occurs through synaptic structure, and retrieval is achieved by letting network dynamics amplify stored correlations that match the cue.

However, naïve Hebbian accumulation has shortcomings. Raw outer-product accumulation indiscriminately increases magnitude with each stored pattern, leading to interference between patterns and uncontrolled growth of weights. Also, Hebb's rule does not by itself ensure that stored patterns are stable fixed points under the network dynamics; that depends on the chosen neuron activation function and the normalization or additional terms in the learning rule. Solving these problems motivated more structured associative memory models (e.g. Hopfield networks) and normalization procedures (covariance rule, subtractive normalization, etc.), which we examine next.

# Bidirectional Associative Memories (BAM) and Hopfield networks

Bidirectional Associative Memory (BAM) and Hopfield networks are two landmark architectures for associative storage and retrieval that formalize Hebbian style storage into dynamical systems with attractor dynamics.

The Hopfield network is a recurrent network of $N$ symmetric units with binary $\{\pm 1\}$ (or bipolar) states. Storage is implemented with a symmetric weight matrix built from outer products of stored patterns $\{ \xi^\mu\}_{\mu=1}^P$:
\[
W = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mu=1}^P \xi^\mu {\xi^\mu}^\top,
\]
typically with zero diagonal ($W_{ii}=0$) to avoid self-feedback. Given a state vector $s\in\{\pm 1\}^N$, asynchronous or synchronous update dynamics use the local field $h = W s$ and apply a sign activation:
\[
s_i \leftarrow \mathrm{sgn}(h_i).
\]
Hopfield showed that with symmetric weights and appropriate activation, this dynamics decreases an energy function
\[
E(s) = -\frac{1}{2} s^\top W s
\]
(or with added constant terms), so the network evolves toward local minima of $E$; stored patterns are (under conditions) attractors. The Hopfield model thus gives an explicit energy landscape interpretation of associative memory: memories are energy minima and retrieval is gradient descent in energy space.

Bidirectional Associative Memory generalizes associative recall to mapping between hetero-associative pairs. BAM consists of two layers, $X$ and $Y$, with connections $W$ from $X$ to $Y$ and $W^\top$ from $Y$ to $X$. Given a set of paired patterns $(x^\mu, y^\mu)$, the BAM weight matrix often uses outer products
\[
W = \sum_{\mu=1}^P x^\mu {y^\mu}^\top.
\]
During retrieval, alternation of updates between the two layers (e.g., $y\leftarrow\mathrm{sgn}(W^\top x)$ then $x\leftarrow\mathrm{sgn}(W y)$) converges to a fixed point that ideally reproduces the stored pair associated with the initial cue. BAM is useful for hetero-association where the stored pair components live in different representation spaces.

Both Hopfield and BAM illustrate how outer-product storage combined with iterative dynamics can retrieve complete patterns from partial cues. The symmetry of weights in Hopfield ensures an energy function exists; asymmetry in BAM is acceptable because the alternating two-layer structure can be seen as energy-decreasing in a combined state space. Stability and convergence properties depend on choices such as activation nonlinearity, normalization, and how many patterns are stored relative to network size.

# Outer-product (covariance) update rules and their properties

The outer-product form of Hebbian updates is simple but not the only useful variant. One key refinement is to use a covariance (or zero-mean) version of the outer-product rule to remove systematic correlations in the data that otherwise act like constant biases. For patterns $x$ drawn from some distribution with mean $\langle x\rangle$, the covariance (centered) update uses
\[
\Delta W \propto (x - \langle x\rangle)(x - \langle x\rangle)^\top,
\]
or for hetero-association between $x$ and $y$,
\[
\Delta W \propto (x - \langle x\rangle)(y - \langle y\rangle)^\top.
\]
Subtracting the mean makes learning sensitive to the fluctuations (signal) around the mean rather than to the mean itself. In associative memory this reduces systematic crosstalk that would bias retrieval.

Properties of outer-product rules can be understood in linear algebraic terms. If one stores orthogonal patterns, outer products produce weight matrices whose action cleanly isolates each pattern: for orthonormal set $\{\xi^\mu\}$,
\[
W = \sum_\mu \xi^\mu {\xi^\mu}^\top
\]
satisfies $W \xi^\nu = \xi^\nu$ and $W$ acts as a projector onto the span of stored patterns. With non-orthogonal patterns, mutual correlations produce interference: applying $W$ to a cue that is a noisy version of $\xi^\nu$ yields not just a scaled copy of $\xi^\nu$ but also contributions from other stored patterns. This is crosstalk.

One can analyze this interference quantitatively. Consider random patterns $\xi^\mu$ with i.i.d components having zero mean and unit variance. Let $W = \tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{\mu=1}^P \xi^\mu {\xi^\mu}^\top$. For a given stored pattern $\xi^1$, the local field when the network state equals $\xi^1$ contains a signal term of size approximately $1$ and a crosstalk (noise) term contributed by the remaining $P-1$ patterns. The variance of the noise scales as $\mathrm{Var}(\text{noise}) \approx \tfrac{P}{N}$. Thus signal-to-noise ratio scales like $\mathrm{SNR} \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{P/N}}$. When $P$ grows too large compared to $N$, noise overwhelms signal and retrieval fails. This scaling argument explains why capacity of naïve outer-product Hebbian storage is limited.

To improve capacity, several modifications are used. The covariance rule (centering), normalizing each outer product by its squared norm, and using sparse binary representations all change the effective scaling of crosstalk and thereby the capacity. Additionally, more sophisticated learning rules, such as the pseudo-inverse rule and the Storkey rule, attempt to orthogonalize new memories with respect to existing ones or to explicitly minimize interference, at the cost of more computation or nonlocal updates.

One important variant is the pseudo-inverse learning rule. If the stored patterns are arranged as columns in matrix $X \in \mathbb{R}^{N\times P}$ (columns $\xi^\mu$), the pseudo-inverse rule sets
\[
W = X (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top,
\]
which is the projector onto the column space of $X$. This $W$ reproduces each stored pattern exactly in the noise-free case (i.e., $W \xi^\mu = \xi^\mu$) even for non-orthogonal patterns, and minimizes least squares reconstruction error. The cost is computing the inverse $(X^\top X)^{-1}$, which is $O(P^3)$ naively, and the update is nonlocal in the sense that adding a new pattern requires recomputation or a rank-one update of the inverse.

Another local but improved rule is Storkey learning, which includes terms that subtract the effect of previously stored patterns in a local manner to reduce interference; it performs better than simple Hebb but is still local and incremental.

Overall, outer-product and covariance rules provide a clear, analytically tractable family of associative memory updates. They offer intuition: memory is stored by adding rank-one matrices; retrieval multiplies a weight matrix by a cue and obtains a linear combination of stored patterns weighted by similarity. Controlling interference and normalizing contributions are central to making these mechanisms work at scale.

# Fast Weight Programmers and the historical lineage

The idea of having synaptic weights that change on fast timescales, separate from slower long-term synaptic changes, has a long history. In biological terms, short-term synaptic plasticity, neuromodulation, and biochemical cascades provide mechanisms by which transient associations can be written into synaptic efficacies for a limited time. In machine learning, the “fast weight” idea was explicitly proposed as a computational mechanism to implement transient memories or to enable networks to rapidly store and retrieve recently observed items.

A modern formalization is the Fast Weight Programmer introduced by Schmidhuber in the early 1990s and elaborated in later works. The general idea is to have two timescales of parameters: slow, long-term parameters that are learned by gradient descent and produce control signals; and fast weights $A_t$ which are rapidly updated at each time step by the network using outer-product (or similar) updates. The slow controller network emits key and value vectors at each step, and the fast-weight matrix accumulates outer products of keys and values:
\[
A_t = \lambda A_{t-1} + \eta k_t v_t^\top,
\]
where $\lambda$ is a decay (forgetting) factor and $\eta$ is a learning rate. During reading, the controller uses a query $q_t$ to retrieve information via multiplication $A_t q_t$, producing a linear combination of stored values weighted by their similarity to the query. In this formulation, outer-product accumulation stores associations between keys and values in the fast weight matrix. The decay factor allows forgetting, making the fast memory short-term and bounded.

Early proposals explored various control architectures for how to generate keys, values, and queries and how to integrate the retrieved result into ongoing computation. Though compelling, these ideas were ahead of their time computationally and were overtaken in popularity by other sequence models. The lineage continues however: attention mechanisms, linear attention, and transformer-style architectures realize many of the same principles, but packaged with efficient normalizations and GPU-friendly dense operations.

Historically, the name Fast Weight Programmer captures the notion that a network with slow learned parameters "programs" the fast weight memory at runtime by issuing outer-product writes. This separates the meta-level (learned policy for writing/reading) from the object-level (fast associative storage). The same conceptual separation appears in modern memory-augmented networks such as Neural Turing Machines or memory networks, but the explicit outer-product fast weight formulation remains a particularly elegant and analytically tractable example.

# Capacity, interference, and normalization in associative memories

Quantifying how many patterns can be stored and reliably retrieved—capacity—is central to understanding associative memories. Capacity depends on the architecture (binary Hopfield, continuous Hopfield, Willshaw, sparse distributed memory), the coding (dense, sparse, binary, real-valued), and the learning rule (Hebb, covariance, pseudo-inverse).

For the classic binary Hopfield network with random bipolar patterns, the asymptotic storage capacity is approximately $P_{\max} \approx 0.138 N$ patterns, where $N$ is the number of neurons. This result comes from statistical physics analyses (replica method) and signal-to-noise approximations: when $P/N$ exceeds a critical load, crosstalk causes retrieval errors and the stored patterns cease to be stable attractors. For sparse binary codes (few ones per pattern), capacity can scale much more favorably in terms of patterns per neuron; for instance, Willshaw networks with extremely sparse patterns have capacity scaling proportional to $N^2 / \log N$ in information-theoretic terms, although practical retrieval and robustness impose additional constraints.

The basic mechanism that limits capacity is interference or crosstalk. As more patterns are stored, the expected overlap (correlation) between a random cue and other stored patterns grows, and these spurious overlaps add up as noise in the retrieval signal. As noted earlier, for a normalized outer-product rule in a dense random code, the variance of crosstalk noise scales as $P/N$, implying $P$ must be $O(N)$ to keep signal-to-noise at a constant level.

Normalization and orthogonalization are standard remedies to reduce interference. Covariance learning (centering) lowers the effective bias created by mean activity; scaling each pattern's contribution by its norm or by a function of its activity levels controls weight growth. More structured remedies attempt to make stored patterns orthogonal in the representation used for storage: one can whiten inputs, learn an embedding that makes patterns more separable, or explicitly construct $W$ as a projector (pseudo-inverse) to perfectly reproduce stored patterns in the noise-free case.

Another perspective is to trade off storage capacity for sparsity and nonlinearity. Sparse codes reduce the expected number of overlapping active components between different patterns, thereby reducing interference. Nonlinear activation functions during retrieval (thresholding) can help suppress weak crosstalk contributions while preserving strong signal components.

Finally, controlled forgetting through decay or limited memory size is important in fast-weight systems: rather than attempting to store an unbounded stream of patterns, the system maintains a fast-weight matrix over a sliding window (via multiplicative decay $\lambda<1$) so that only the most recent items remain accessible. This limits interference by bounding the effective $P$ in the fast memory.

# Modern instantiations: fast-weight attention, retrieval dynamics

Modern neural architectures have rediscovered and refined associative storage ideas. The attention mechanism that underpins transformers can be interpreted as a normalized, soft, and efficient outer-product associative memory. Consider keys $k_i\in\mathbb{R}^d$ and values $v_i\in\mathbb{R}^m$ produced for stored items $i=1,\dots,P$. Define the accumulated associative matrix
\[
A = \sum_{i=1}^P k_i v_i^\top.
\]
Given a query $q$, the linear retrieval $A^\top q = \sum_i v_i (k_i^\top q)$ returns a weighted sum of values with unnormalized weights equal to similarity scores $k_i^\top q$. Modern attention adds a softmax normalization to produce convex weights:
\[
\text{Attn}(q) = \sum_{i=1}^P v_i \,\frac{\exp(k_i^\top q)}{\sum_j \exp(k_j^\top q)}.
\]
This softmax-attention is equivalent to a differentiable, normalized associative retrieval rule: it keeps the outer-product storage idea but replaces raw inner products with exponentially reweighed normalized similarities, which improves robustness and ensures weights sum to one.

Beyond the standard quadratic-memory attention, linearized attention variants seek to compute attention in $O(P)$ time and $O(d^2)$ memory by algebraically rearranging the operations. One common trick is to use a feature map $\phi(\cdot)$ such that $\exp(k_i^\top q)$ is approximated by $\phi(k_i)^\top \phi(q)$, allowing
\[
A = \sum_i \phi(k_i) v_i^\top, \qquad \text{and}\qquad A^\top \phi(q) = \sum_i v_i (\phi(k_i)^\top \phi(q)),
\]
which can be computed by first forming $\sum_i \phi(k_i) v_i^\top$ and then multiplying by $\phi(q)$. This is precisely a fast-weight-style outer-product memory that is incrementally updatable and is compatible with streaming data.

An important theoretical development connected classical Hopfield networks and attention: modern continuous Hopfield networks and “transformer attention as Hopfield networks” results show that softmax attention implements gradient-based retrieval in an energy landscape. Ramsauer et al. and others demonstrated that attention with stored patterns as keys and values corresponds to fixed points of an energy function, and that the number of stable states (capacity) of modern Hopfield variants grows exponentially in the dimensionality of the key space, under suitable assumptions. Practically, attention mechanisms achieve very large practical capacity because keys are high-dimensional continuous vectors that can be learned to be highly separable, and because softmax sharply selects the best-matching values rather than averaging many weak matches.

Retrieval dynamics in modern systems can be single-shot (one matrix-vector product as in attention) or iterative. Iterative retrieval can refine an initial read, using the retrieved vector to re-query the memory or to update fast weights, leading to content-addressable refinement similar to running a Hopfield network over several steps. Such dynamics can be implemented explicitly via recurrence or implicitly via multiple attention layers. Iteration helps in disambiguating between similar items and can be used to implement associative chaining (retrieve item A then use it to cue item B).

Fast-weight mechanisms also appear in learned controllers that write into and read from associative matrices during a sequence. Recent work revisits learned fast-weight controllers that compute write keys and values with a learned amortized rule, combining outer-product accumulation with decay and adaptive write strengths. These systems are trained end-to-end and can implement complex patterns of short-term memory, binding, and variable storage with high capacity and flexible retrieval.

In summary, modern attention and fast-weight instantiations put classical outer-product associative ideas on practical footing: learned high-dimensional keys reduce interference, softmax normalization focuses retrieval, efficient algebraic rearrangements make computation and updates tractable, and iteration or learned decay controls memory lifetime. The conceptual continuity is striking: early Hebbian and fast-weight proposals described associative programming of transient synapses; today’s attention mechanisms are, in many respects, the practical, scalable descendants of those proposals.

Concluding remarks

Associative memory bridges neuroscience, statistical physics, and modern deep learning. The outer-product is the simplest and most expressive primitive for associatively storing relationships between vectors, and the central challenges—interference, capacity limits, and stability—have driven the development of covariance normalization, pseudo-inverse corrections, sparse coding, decay-based forgetting, and ultimately modern attention mechanisms. Fast-weight programmers provided the early computational blueprint that has reappeared in modern attention and transformer architectures: slow parameters produce keys and values, and a fast, transient associative store accumulates outer products to be queried later. Understanding the linear algebra and dynamics of these mechanisms—how signal and crosstalk scale, how normalization reshapes retrieval, and how iterative dynamics refine memory—gives you the tools to read current research that blends associative ideas with learned representations and to design systems that exploit associative memory effectively.

Chapter 12: Key-Value Memory Architectures and Retrieval
